                                                                    UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS ASYMPTOTICS
                                                                       OF WEIL–PETERSSON VOLUMES

                                                                                   ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


                                                   Abstract. Let Vg,n be the Weil–Petersson volume of the moduli space of hyperbolic surfaces of
                                                   genus g with n cusps. The fixed-n large-genus expansion of Mirzakhani and Zograf has correction
                                                                                                                                                 √
                                                   terms polynomial in n of degree 2i at order g −i , and ceases to be uniform when n is of order g. We
arXiv:2610.35492v3 [math.DG] 29 Oct 2026




                                                   prove an asymptotic expansion in powers of 1/g that holds uniformly in all n ≥ 0, with coefficients
                                                   analytic in a density parameter that ranges over a compact interval, including the limits n/g → 0
                                                   and n/g → ∞. Its leading term is explicit, contains no undetermined constant and√has relative
                                                                                                             √
                                                   error O(1/g); truncation at an order proportional to g gives relative error O(e−a g ) for some
                                                   a > 0. For fixed n this confirms Zograf’s conjecture that the constant in the Mirzakhani–Zograf
                                                                       √
                                                   asymptotics is 1/ π. We also determine, at every cusp density, the limits of the volume ratios
                                                   Vg,n /Vg−1,n+2 and Vg,n+1 /(2gVg,n ) and, in all degrees, the asymptotics of the ratios of adjacent
                                                   coefficients of the one-boundary volume polynomial; this answers two questions of Mirzakhani. The
                                                   proof deforms mixed κ1 –ψ intersection numbers into pure ψ intersection numbers along a family
                                                   whose recursion has nonnegative coefficients, fixes the overall constant through Painlevé I without
                                                   assuming resurgence, and recovers individual volumes by a local limit theorem in the number of
                                                                                                                                                √
                                                   cusps. As an application, the spectral gap of a Weil–Petersson random surface with n ≫ g cusps
                                                   tends to zero in probability; this removes the restriction n/g → 0 from a theorem of Shen and Wu.

                                            1. Introduction
  Let Vg,n (L1 , . . . , Ln ) denote the Weil–Petersson volume of the moduli space of genus-g hyperbolic
surfaces with n labeled boundary components of lengths Li . We use the orbifold convention and put
                                                                       Z
                                                                1                 3g−3+n
                  Vg,n = Vg,n (0, . . . , 0),  Vg,n (L) =                        ωWP     .
                                                          (3g − 3 + n)! Mg,n (L)
We assume 2g − 2 + n > 0. A boundary component of length zero is a cusp. These volumes
are computed by Mirzakhani’s recursion [61], are generating functions for intersection numbers on
Mg,n [62], and control the geometry of random hyperbolic surfaces of large genus [64]; see [84] for a
survey, and [17] for a survey comparing Weil–Petersson and Masur–Veech volumes.
    Precise asymptotics are known in two limits. For fixed n as g → ∞, Mirzakhani obtained growth
estimates and the limits of the basic volume ratios [64], and Mirzakhani and Zograf obtained a
complete asymptotic expansion [66]. For fixed g as n → ∞, the form of the asymptotics was
predicted by Itzykson (see [90, Remark 3]). In genus zero, where Zograf had given a recursion for
the volumes [89], Kaufmann, Manin and Zagier proved this prediction and expressed the exponential
growth rate through the first zero of J0 [48]. Zograf then expressed the generating function of the
volumes in every genus through the Kontsevich–Witten free energy at shifted times [90], and Manin
and Zograf proved the asymptotics for every fixed genus [58, Theorem 6.1].
    These two limiting descriptions do not by themselves give a uniform approximation when both
parameters grow. By [66, Theorem 1.2], the coefficient of g −i in the fixed-n expansion is a polynomial
in n of degree 2i with leading term (−1)i n2i /(i! (2π 2 )i ). The expansion is thus effectively a series in
                                                                                          √
n2 /g, whose leading terms sum formally to exp(−n2 /(2π 2 g)). Once n is of order g, all its terms
are of comparable size, so no fixed truncation remains a uniform approximation.
    Main results. We prove a single asymptotic expansion of Vg,n as g → ∞ that is uniform in
n ≥ 0. Its leading term is explicit and contains no undetermined constant, and its relative remainder
is uniform from bounded cusp counts to cusp counts growing arbitrarily fast with the genus. The
main consequences are the following.
• Absolute volumes      (Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2). The relative error is O(1/g) uniformly in n,
                √                                                    √
   and O(e−a g ) after truncation at an order proportional to g. For fixed n this identifies the
                                                                                       √
   constant in the Mirzakhani–Zograf asymptotic formula [66, Theorem 1.2] as 1/ π, as conjectured
   by Zograf [91].
• Volume ratios (Theorem 1.3). The ratios Vg,n /Vg−1,n+2 , Vg,n+1 /(2gVg,n ) and Vg,n    2 /(V
                                                                                               g,n−1 Vg,n+1 )
   have explicit limits at every cusp density, with rate O(1/g). The first limit answers Mirzakhani’s
   question on the asymptotics of Vg,n /Vg−1,n+2 when n grows with g [64, Section 1.4]. The third
   settles the remaining case of a question of Shen and Wu [79, Question 3], who asked whether
     2 /(V
   Vg,n     g,n−1 Vg,n+1 ) → 1 as g + n → ∞.
• Coefficients and the diagonal chain (Theorems 1.4 and 1.5). We determine the ratios of adjacent
   coefficients of the one-boundary volume polynomial over all degrees, including degrees that grow
   with the genus; this answers Mirzakhani’s second question [64, Section 1.4]. We also determine, to
   logarithmic precision and including bounded genus, the whole chain VG,0 , VG−1,2 , . . . , V0,2G , about
   whose behavior she remarked in the same section that little was known.
• Random surfaces with many cusps (Subsection 1.6). Combined with the vanishing-gap argument
   of Shen and Wu [79], the volume ratios show that the spectral gap tends to zero in probability
                  √
   whenever n/ g → ∞; the new range n ≍ g completes the negative answer to their question
   whether such surfaces have a uniform positive spectral gap [79, Question 2]. The volume ratios
                                                                                                   √
   also determine the statistics of short geodesics and of the systole at every density above g, and
                                                                                       √
   the tight length spectrum for every cusp sequence. At the critical scale n ≍ g we identify the
   full primitive length spectrum; this last result needs only the zero-density case of the volume-ratio
   limits.
4                                      ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


   Method. The proof has three ingredients. A one-parameter deformation turns the alternating
formula for mixed κ1 –ψ intersection numbers into a recursion with nonnegative coefficients. The
normalization is fixed at the pure-ψ end of the deformation through Painlevé I, with no resurgence
assumption. Finally, a local limit theorem in the number of cusps, valid at both ends of the parameter
range, passes from generating functions to individual volumes.
1.1. The cusp-density parameter. All our formulas are expressed through a single real parameter.
We first define it and then explain, heuristically, why it is the right parameter; the proofs come later.
   The genus-zero relation. Let

xc on [0, y0 ]; write Y (x) for the inverse function. Consider the normalized cusp generating functions

So, under the cusp weight x, the number of additional cusps is about hλ(Y )/2 ≈ gλ(Y ). To recover
a single volume Vg,n , one chooses Y so that the leading approximation to this mean equals n − 1,
that is, λ(Y ) = 2(n − 1)/(2g − 1), and estimates the probability of the value k = n − 1 by a local
limit theorem. In particular λ(Y ) is, to leading order, the cusp density n/g, and we use Y and λ
interchangeably. This heuristic produces the leading term in Theorem 1.1. As n/g increases from 0
to ∞, the parameter Y increases from 0 to y0 and the cusp weight x from 0 to xc .
   The difficulty is to make this heuristic rigorous with relative errors uniform in n. The proof
of (1.4) uses a differential equation in Y whose integration constant is fixed at the endpoint Y = y0 ,
and the passage from the generating function to its coefficients needs Fourier bounds that survive at
both ends of the parameter interval. Subsection 1.7 describes these steps.
   A matrix-model interpretation. The cusp weight is a defect weight in Jackiw–Teitelboim (JT)
gravity, and Y locates the edge of the corresponding spectral density. At the endpoint Y = y0 the
square-root edge becomes cubic. Subsection 15.1 gives the precise dictionary with the defect-deformed
matrix model of Maxfield–Turiaci and Witten [59, 82, 22]. In this language the endpoint corresponds
to the (2, 3) critical point, and this is the point at which our normalized recursion reduces to pure ψ
intersection numbers. The interpretation does not by itself control the remainders.

   The uniformity in n is the main point: Theorem 1.1 replaces the fixed-n series in n2 /g by an
expansion in 1/g alone. The same estimate applies when n is fixed, when n is proportional to g,
and when n/g → ∞. In the first regime the saddle is near x = 0; as n/g → ∞ it tends to the
radius of convergence xc of the fixed-genus generating functions of Manin and Zograf. In this way a
single formula joins the two classical regimes. The order g −1 is sharp for this choice of leading term
(Theorem 11.1); including the first correction (D.24), or (11.7) for n = 0, gives error O(g −2 ). The
proof is completed in Section 11.
                                                                                                  √
   For fixed n, Mirzakhani and Zograf proved that Vg,n ∼ C(2g − 3 + n)! (4π 2 )2g−3+n / g for a
universal constant
             √      C > 0 [66, Theorem 1.2]. On the basis of numerical data, Zograf had conjectured
that C = 1/ π [91]; see also [66, Remark 1.3]. The same value appears in physical predictions. Saad,
Shenker and Stanford obtained the unmarked large-genus prediction from the JT matrix integral          √
under a resurgence assumption [77, Section 5.6]. Okuyama and Sakai extracted the constant 1/ π,
together with first corrections for the coefficients of Vg,1 (L) at each fixed degree, numerically from the
KdV equation [71, Section 2.4, equation (89)]. Kimura conjectured the corresponding asymptotics
of Vg,n (L) from JT gravity [50, equation (28)], and Eynard, Garcia-Failde, Gregori, Lewański and
Schiappa derived it from the nonperturbative topological recursion, assuming that the generating
series of Weil–Petersson volumes is resurgent [25, Section 6.1, equation (6.7)].
   Our argument uses no resurgence assumption. We fix the normalization at the endpoint Y = y0
of the parameter interval, where the problem reduces to pure ψ intersection numbers and only the
single sequence ⟨τ23g−3 ⟩g enters. An identity of Itzykson and Zuber [43] expresses this sequence
through the formal power-series solution of Painlevé I, whose large-order asymptotics follow from
the results of Joshi–Kitaev, Kapaev, and Garoufalidis, Its, Kapaev and Mariño [46, 47, 27]. The
leading term and the first correction of this sequence also appear as a special case of the uniform
asymptotics for ψ intersection numbers announced by Guo, Yang and Zagier [32]; we derive both
from Painlevé I in Subsections 7.3 and C.9, and use [32] only as an independent check. Comparison
with the Mirzakhani–Zograf expansion gives the following corollary.

Section 9 proves the uniform limits directly from a local limit theorem. The inverse-genus error
follows from the sharper coefficient estimates, as shown in Section 11.1. Before this theorem, the
                                                                                               √
limit of Cg,n was known for n = o(g), as described above, and the limit Bg,n → 1 for n = o( g), by
the absolute estimate [66, Theorem 1.8]. Theorem 1.3 removes these restrictions and gives the rate
O(g −1 ) uniformly in n.
   The statement on Dg,n answers a question of Shen and Wu [79, Question 3], which asks whether
Dg,n → 1 as g + n → ∞. As they observe, the answer is affirmative when n = o(g), by [66,
Lemma 5.1], and when g is fixed, by [58, Theorem 6.1]; they also proved 1/2 − π 2 /20 ≤ lim inf Dg,n ≤
lim sup Dg,n ≤ 1 for every cusp sequence [79, Proposition 36]. Theorem 1.3 supplies the remaining
range, in which g → ∞ and n/g ̸→ 0. Combined with the fixed-genus case, it gives Dg,n → 1
whenever g + n → ∞.
1.4. Adjacent coefficients. Mirzakhani’s second question [64, Section 1.4] concerns adjacent
coefficients when the degree grows with the genus. We write the one-boundary volume polynomial
in the normalization of [64],
                                         N
                                         X       ag,k
                            Vg,1 (L) =                   L2k ,   N = 3g − 2.
                                               (2k + 1)!
                                         k=0

The normalized intersection bracket bg,k = [τk ]g,1 uses the expansion in 2L, and hence bg,k = 4k ag,k
(Section 4). For fixed k, its adjacent ratio tends to one [64, Theorem 3.5]; the corresponding
ratio ag,k /ag,k+1 tends to four. Thus the limit one stated in [64, Section 1.4] refers to the bracket
normalization, which is also the one used in [55].

The density (2k + 1)/(2g − 1) keeps the exact codimension m in the saddle equation; this is why
the last formula also covers bounded positive m. Section 13 proves the uniform equivalent, and
Appendix E proves the stated error. On the range k ≥ εg, for each fixed ε ∈ (0, 3), the stronger
error Oε (g −1/4 ) holds, again including bounded positive codimension.
   For each fixed codimension, Liu and Xu obtained a complete expansion of the coefficients relative to
the top one [55, Theorem 1.2], a partial answer to Mirzakhani’s question. The bounded-codimension
case of Theorem 1.4 agrees with their result after the change of normalization. What is new is
uniformity over all degrees, including degrees and codimensions that grow with the genus.
   The trigonometric saddle equation is the stationary equation of an instanton prediction from
physics; see Subsections 1.8.2 and 13.7.
1.5. The diagonal chain. Along the chain VG,0 , VG−1,2 , . . . , V0,2G the Euler characteristic is con-
stant, and genus is traded for pairs of cusps. Mirzakhani noted that the adjacent ratios at the two
ends of this chain follow from the fixed-n and fixed-g asymptotics, but that little was known about
the behavior of the whole sequence [64, Section 1.4]. Let Y (u) correspond to λ = 2u/(1 − u) and
define
                         Z t
                                 4∆(Y (u))

The second statement determines each entry of the chain up to a factor exp(o(G)), uniformly in its

genus tends to infinity, Theorem 1.1 gives far more precise information; the point of Theorem 1.5 is
that it also covers bounded genus.
1.6. Short geodesics and small eigenvalues. Volume ratios control the geometry of random
surfaces through Mirzakhani’s integration formula [61], which expresses the expected number of short
curves of a given topological type through volumes of the surfaces obtained by cutting along them.
Let PWP
      g,n be the probability measure obtained by normalizing Weil–Petersson volume on Mg,n , and
let ∆X be the nonnegative Laplace–Beltrami operator of a surface X. The short geometry changes
                  √              √
at the scale n ≍ g: for n = o( g) the spectral gap is bounded below with high probability [79,
Theorem 3], whereas above this scale it closes.

 Corollary 1.6 (Vanishing spectral gap). Suppose that g → ∞ and that the integer cusp counts
              √
 satisfy n(g)/ g → ∞. Then, for every ε > 0,
                         PWP
                          g,n(g) {X : ∆X has an eigenvalue in (0, ε)} −→ 1.

This corollary is an application of Shen–Wu’s vanishing-gap argument [79, Theorem 1 and Proposi-
tion 35] with a stronger volume input. Shen and Wu asked whether a generic surface in Mg,n has
                                                                           √
a uniform positive spectral gap when n grows significantly faster than g [79, Question 2]. They
                              √
proved that it does not for g ≪ n ≪ g, noted that Zograf’s deterministic bound [88] gives the
same conclusion for n/g → ∞, and expected that the restriction n/g → 0 in their theorem could be
removed, which would settle the question. The remaining range is n ≍ g. As Shen and Wu point out,
their short-curve argument covers this range once the adjacent-volume limit Dg,n−1 → 1 is known,
that is, once their question [79, Question 3] has an affirmative answer there. Theorem 1.3 supplies
this limit, and Subsection 14.1 applies their argument with this input. Corollary 1.6 therefore
completes the negative answer to [79, Question 2].
   Short geodesics and the systole. Set qg = n(n − 1)/(8gC(n/g)) ≍ n2 /(g + n). For small L,
the expected number of geodesics of length at most L that cut off two cusps is asymptotic to qg L2
                        −1/2
(Proposition 14.2), so qg    is the natural length scale of the shortest curves. Under the hypotheses
of Corollary 1.6, and assuming in addition that n/g → λ ∈ [0, ∞], Theorem 14.1 identifies the
Poisson process formed by all primitive closed geodesics on this scale, together with the joint law
of the systole and the number of cusps it cuts off. With probability tending to one, the shortest
geodesics separate off a genus-zero subsurface that contains only cusps. At limiting cusp density
λ, the curves that cut off k cusps have rescaled intensity 2wk (xλ )t dt, where xλ = X(Yλ ) and the
weights wk are given in (14.3). At zero density only curves cutting off two cusps survive in the limit,
whereas at positive density every k ≥ 2 has positive intensity.
   Many small eigenvalues. Counting disjoint short pairs of pants and applying the localized
min–max argument of Hide and Thomas [41], Corollary 14.3 gives a growing number of eigenvalues
tending to zero, with an explicit bound on the jth eigenvalue whenever j → ∞ and j = o(qg ).
   The tight length spectrum. The same estimates determine a different part of the length
spectrum for every cusp sequence n = n(g) ≥ 0. A tight geodesic is shortest in its homotopy
                                                                                        q class after
filling all cusps. In Theorem 14.4, the primitive tight lengths, multiplied by α(n/g) = 2r(Yn/g )/π,
converge to a Poisson process with intensity
                                             cosh t − 1
                                                        dt.
                                                  t
This extends the near-critical Boltzmann theorem of Budd and Lions [13] to arbitrary prescribed
cusp counts, using their tight integration formula and geometric moment argument. The scale
satisfies α(0) = 1 and α(λ) ≍ λ−1/4 as λ → ∞.
10                                    ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG

                                 √
   The critical scale. At n/ g → c ∈ (0, ∞), the full ordinary primitive length spectrum has a
different limit (Theorem 14.6). It is the sum of an independent Poisson background of intensity
(cosh t − 1) dt/t and the complete primitive spectra of two-cusp pairs of pants. Their waist lengths
form a Poisson process of intensity c2 sinh(s/2) ds/(8π 2 ). Shen–Wu’s short pants count already
exhibits this waist intensity [79, Lemmas 28 and 33]; the full limit retains the correlations between all
lengths in each pair of pants and is not Poisson. Besides the volume bounds of [66, 79], this critical
limit uses only the zero-density genus-ratio limit Vg,n /Vg−1,n+2 → 1 from Theorem 1.3, rather than
deducing this two-sided limit from the volume bounds of [66, 79]. Its additional content is the joint
pants decomposition, including the nonsimple curves.
1.7. Strategy of the proof. The heuristic of Subsection 1.1 has two parts: the approximation (1.4)
of Φ′g , uniform in the cusp weight, and the passage from Φ′g to one of its coefficients. We describe
how each part is proved.
   Mirzakhani’s volumes are intersection numbers of κ1 and ψ classes, and κ1 can be expressed
through pure ψ intersections [48, 58]. The resulting formula (4.4) is an alternating sum over the
number of additional ψ insertions, and asymptotics for its terms do not control the cancellation.
Our first step replaces it by a recursion with nonnegative coefficients.
   (i) A positive deformation. Moving along the string flow by a time Y replaces the alternating
        weights −X (k) (0) by the derivatives −X (k) (Y ); see (4.5). At Y = 0 the resulting shifted
        amplitudes are the Weil–Petersson brackets. After a natural rescaling they become, at Y = y0 ,
        pure ψ intersection numbers with insertions of τ2 . The Dijkgraaf–Verlinde–Verlinde equations
        turn into a recursion (4.8) whose coefficients are the Taylor coefficients of 1/KY , and these are
        nonnegative for every Y (Section 2). Comparing this recursion with an explicit solvable model,
        the harmonic model √(4.11), gives two-sided bounds that are uniform in Y and in all degrees
        but lose a factor eO( g) (Proposition 5.1). This step uses no information about volumes.
  (ii) Ratios, a differential equation and the normalization. For a fixed number of distinguished
                                                                            (p)
        markings, the dilaton equation expresses the Y -derivative of log Φg (X(Y )) through amplitudes
        with one more marking, and the KdV equation relates genus g to genus g − 1 with two more
        markings. Positivity makes the amplitudes decrease as a degree increases, and this upgrades the
        coarse bounds of step (i) to relative errors O(1/g) for both ratios (Proposition 6.2). Integrating
        the resulting differential equation in Y gives (1.4) up to a factor independent of Y . This
        factor is fixed at Y = y0 , where only ⟨τ23g−3 ⟩g enters and Painlevé I supplies the asymptotics
        (Section 7). Iterating the same equations gives all higher corrections, with remainders controlled
        as the order grows (Section 8, with proofs in Appendix C).
 (iii) From generating functions to coefficients. An expansion of a generating function does not by
                                                                (p)
        itself approximate its coefficients. We show that Φg is (1/X ′ (Y (x)))2g−2+p times a power
        series with nonnegative coefficients (Lemma 9.1). This factorization gives Fourier bounds on
        the circle |z| = x, and hence a local limit theorem for the cusp count, uniformly from x near
        0 to x near xc (Proposition 9.2). Near x = 0 the cusp count is nearly Poisson. A change
        of variables of the type studied by Temme [80] then gives an expansion around the Poisson
        law that remains regular for bounded cusp counts (Section 10, with proofs in Appendix D).
        Together these estimates yield Theorem 1.1 (Section 11).
1.8. Relation to previous work. We group earlier work into three threads: the geometry of
moduli spaces and curve counting, the asymptotics of volumes and intersection numbers, and short
geodesics and spectra of random hyperbolic surfaces. The last subsection compares the present paper
with contemporaneous work on Zograf’s constant.
1.8.1. Moduli spaces, volumes and curve counting. Weil–Petersson volumes play two roles in this
paper: they are generating functions for intersection numbers, and they compute probabilities
                            UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                       11


for random hyperbolic surfaces. Penner developed a combinatorial method for computing these
volumes [72]. Building on McShane’s identity [60], Mirzakhani derived a recursion that computes
Vg,n (L) as a polynomial in the squared boundary lengths [61]; its coefficients are intersection numbers
of tautological classes on Mg,n [62], with the symplectic normalization established by Wolpert [83].
Mulase and Safnuk recast the recursion as Virasoro constraints and showed that the generating
function of mixed κ1 –ψ intersections is a one-parameter solution of the Korteweg–de Vries (KdV)
hierarchy [69]; Eynard and Orantin identified it with the loop equations of a matrix model [26]. The
identities expressing mixed κ1 –ψ intersections through pure ψ intersections, which are the starting
point of Section 4, are due to Kaufmann, Manin and Zagier and to Manin and Zograf [48, 58].
Related exact formulas include the tight-volume recursions of Budd and Zonneveld [14], the KdV
boundary-addition identities and fixed-genus Bessel singularity studied by Johnson [44], and the
Virasoro-theoretic proof by Xiong and Zhou of the intersection-number and cohomological-field-
theory descriptions of topological recursion, including variation of the spectral-curve function [87,
Theorem 1.1 and Section 5.7]. These are exact identities; obtaining uniform remainder estimates
from them is a separate analytic problem.
   Mirzakhani’s integration formula [61] expresses the average number of simple multicurves of a
prescribed type in terms of volumes of the surfaces obtained by cutting along them. After division
by Vg,n , these formulas become expectations under Weil–Petersson probability, so relative volume
estimates control geometric probabilities. Mirzakhani used this connection to study random surfaces
at large genus [64], and Mirzakhani and Petri used joint curve counts to obtain Poisson length
statistics for closed surfaces [65]. A different curve-counting limit fixes the surface and lets the
length bound tend to infinity. Mirzakhani’s counting theorem assigns frequencies to simple-curve
topological types in that limit [63]. Delecroix, Goujard, Zograf and Zorich related these frequencies to
intersection numbers and Masur–Veech volumes [20]; Ren obtained the large-genus ratio of separating
to nonseparating frequencies with many cusps [75]. Those frequencies are topological quantities,
whereas the processes studied here count short curves on a random surface as its topology varies.
   Masur–Veech volumes are the flat counterpart of Weil–Petersson volumes, and their large-genus
theory has developed in parallel. Eskin and Zorich conjectured the large-genus asymptotics of
Masur–Veech volumes of strata of Abelian differentials [23]; Aggarwal proved them for all strata [1],
and Chen, Möller, Sauvaget and Zagier expressed these volumes through intersection numbers on the
strata and evaluated their large-genus limits [16]. Rafi and Zorich recently determined the large-n
asymptotics of Masur–Veech volumes of strata of quadratic differentials with many simple poles at
fixed genus [74].
1.8.2. Asymptotics of volumes and intersection numbers. The fixed-genus regime was recalled at the
beginning of the introduction. Here we discuss large-genus estimates at fixed cusp count, coefficients
of growing degree, pure ψ intersection numbers and predictions from physics.
   Fixed cusp counts. Grushevsky [29] and, for closed surfaces, Schumacher and Trapani [78]
showed that for fixed n the volumes grow like (2g)! up to factors cg ; see the account in [64, Section 1.4].
After Mirzakhani’s estimates [64], Mirzakhani and Zograf obtained the expansion to every fixed
order [66, Theorem 1.2]. They showed that its coefficients are polynomials in n with coefficients in
Q[π −2 , π 2 ] and observed numerically that only Q[π −2 ] occurs [66, Theorem 1.2 and Remark 1.3];
their leading term also holds when n2 /g → 0 [66, Theorem 1.8], and their cusp-ratio estimate has
error O(n/g) for n ≥ 1 [66, Lemma 5.1]. Their first unmarked correction enters (11.5). He, Wu
                                                                                                  √
and Xue obtained first-order volume-ratio expansions with error O((1 + n2 )/g 2 ) for n = o( g) [36,
Theorem 4.11]. Anantharaman and Monk expanded the volume polynomials at large genus [4].
                                                                                             √
Guillermit sharpened the coefficient bounds and treated boundary lengths of order g for fixed
n [30, Theorems 2, 4, 5 and Remark 3]. For fixed n, her order-j bound (Cn j)j grows more slowly
with j than our bound involving (j!)2 ; our constants, however, are uniform over all cusp counts.
Hide, Macera and Thomas gave bounds explicit in the expansion order, cusp count and degrees [38,
12                                   ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


Theorem 4.2, A1–A4]. Their genus must exceed an order-dependent multiple of the cusp count, and
the errors grow polynomially in the cusp count or degrees, so these bounds do not yield the relative
remainder of Theorem 1.1 at positive cusp density.
   Growing degrees. Wu and Xue established effective   √ upper bounds for normalized intersection
numbers when some degrees are large compared with 2g − 2 + n, together with bounds for volumes
with large boundary lengths [85]. These bounds control individual coefficients and volumes, whereas
the adjacent-ratio asymptotics of Theorem 1.4 require relative control across neighboring degrees. A
further difficulty is that a bracket may itself be small, so that an additive error polynomial in the
degree gives no relative control. We use the Mirzakhani–Zograf bracket-difference estimates near
the lowest degrees; the full degree range requires the harmonic comparison and the log-concavity
argument of Section 13.
   Pure ψ intersection numbers. Motivated by Masur–Veech volumes of quadratic differentials,
Delecroix, Goujard, Zograf and Zorich conjectured uniform large-genus asymptotics for pure ψ
                                                                                      √
intersections (Appendix E of the arXiv version of [20]). Aggarwal proved them for o( g) insertions [2],
and Guo and Yang gave a different proof for at most C log g insertions, for every fixed C, based
on an explicit formula for the n-point generating series [31, Theorem 1]. For a fixed number of
insertions, Eynard, Garcia-Failde, Giacchetto, Gregori and Lewański obtained resurgent expansions
to every fixed order, uniformly in the degrees [24, Theorem 3.13].
   Our comparison needs only fixed positive upper and lower bounds for the normalized pure-ψ
intersections. Appendix B proves these bounds by a scalar induction, adapting the recursion estimates
of Guo, Yang and Zagier [32] and using the published two-point bound of Delecroix, Goujard, Zograf
and Zorich [19]. The absolute constant is fixed by the single sequence ⟨τ23g−3 ⟩g through the Itzykson–
Zuber identity and Painlevé I. To match the expansions at the pure-ψ endpoint when the truncation
order grows with g, we use the exact identity [43, equation (6.10)], the zero-instanton contour formula
of Garoufalidis, Its, Kapaev and Mariño [27], and the Borel representation of Costin, Costin and
Huang [18]. Alameddine, Marchal, Nikolaev and Orantin recently solved the direct monodromy
problem for the Borel-summed zero-parameter solution of Painlevé I and identified it with the
tritronquée solutions [3]; we do not need this. Borot, Eynard and Giacchetto use comparison with
Painlevé I amplitudes to bound topological-recursion correlators, uniformly in genus and number of
markings [9, Main Theorem and Section 1.3]. Their bounds concern the growth of the correlators
themselves, whereas the factorial bounds in Theorem 1.1 concern the order of the inverse-genus
expansion after division by an explicit leading term.
   Instanton predictions. Eynard, Garcia-Failde, Gregori, Lewański and Schiappa derived large-
genus asymptotics for ordinary Weil–Petersson volumes under a resurgence assumption [25]. Griguolo,
Papalini, Russo and Seminara predicted the coefficients of ordinary Weil–Petersson volumes from
instanton contributions [28, Section 3.3], Johnson and Rodrigues wrote down the corresponding
one-boundary contour integral [45, equation (4.22)], and Hatsuda, Matsumoto and Okuyama
constructed transseries solutions of the KdV equation that governs the genus expansion of the JT
partition function, whose leading nonperturbative sector reproduces the instanton terms [34]. After
a change of conventions, the denominator of this integral is our harmonic model at Y = 0, and its
stationary equation is the saddle equation of Theorem 1.4 (Subsection 13.7). Unlike the leading
factor sinh(L/2)/(L/2) at bounded boundary length, this integral retains the dependence of the
coefficients on their degree up to the top degree. Theorem 1.4 establishes the resulting asymptotic
formula for the true adjacent coefficient ratios, uniformly over all degrees, from exact intersection
identities and estimates; no resurgent expansion is assumed.
1.8.3. Short geodesics and spectra of random hyperbolic surfaces. For closed surfaces, Mirzakhani
established a positive spectral gap with high probability [64, Section 4.5]. Wu and Xue [86] and,
independently, Lipnowski and Wright [54] raised the lower bound to 3/16 − ε. Anantharaman and
Monk then obtained 2/9−ε, first in the original version of the first part of their Friedman–Ramanujan
                           UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                 13


series [5, Theorem 10.1] and now in a separate article [7], and then 1/4 − ε [6, Theorem 1.6]; Hide,
Macera and Thomas gave a polynomial rate of approach to 1/4 [38]. These results concern closed
surfaces sampled with Weil–Petersson measure. In a different model, uniformly random covers of
a fixed surface, Magee, Naud and Puder obtained the relative spectral gap 3/16 − ε for covers of
a compact surface [57]. For covers of a fixed cusped surface, Hide and Magee proved that no new
eigenvalues appear below 1/4 − ε, and deduced that there are closed surfaces of genus tending to
infinity with first eigenvalue tending to 1/4 [39]. Growing cusp counts introduce the separating
pants mechanism studied by Shen and Wu [79]. See [68, 73] for surveys of spectral gaps of random
hyperbolic surfaces.
                                        √
   The transition occurs at the scale g. Below it, Hide proved [37, Theorem 1.3] that for n =
O(g α ) with 0 ≤ α < 1/2 there is, with probability tending to one, no nonzero eigenvalue below
1/4 − ((2α + 1)/4)2 − ε. Shen and Wu proved a uniform positive lower bound for the spectral
                                   √
gap throughout the range n = o( g) [79, Theorem 3], and He, Wu and Xue improved the bound
for n = O(g α ) to 1/4 − (6(1 − α))−2 − ε, which tends to 5/36 − ε as α → 1/2 [36, Theorem 1.2].
          √
At n ≍ g, Shen and Wu’s result [79, Theorem 2] describes the small-value distribution of the
Cheeger constant, not a limiting law for Laplace eigenvalues. Above this scale the spectral gap
vanishes in probability (Corollary 1.6), which answers negatively the question of Shen and Wu
                                                     √
whether a uniform spectral gap persists when n/ g → ∞ [79, Question 2]. This is a statement
about the Weil–Petersson distribution: Guo, Hua and Shen construct surfaces with n/g tending to
any prescribed positive limit and a uniform spectral gap [33].
   Poisson limits and small eigenvalues at fixed genus were obtained by Hide and Thomas [41, 40];
their recent deterministic linear count of eigenvalues below 1/4 [42] concerns a fixed threshold
rather than the vanishing thresholds of Corollary 14.3. For closed surfaces the picture is different:
Monk proved that, with probability tending to one, a Weil–Petersson random closed surface has
                                                                           √
O(g(log g)−3/4 ) eigenvalues below 1/4 [67, Theorem 4]. In the range g ≪ n ≪ g, the second-
moment volume inputs for our short-curve and small-eigenvalue results already follow from [79];
what the uniform volume theorem adds is a single intensity valid across all cusp densities as the
genus tends to infinity.
   Budd and Lions studied the tight length spectrum in a near-critical Boltzmann model, where
the cusp count is random [13]. They asked whether their limit persists for other cusp weights and
under the fixed-count measures [13, Open Question 1.6]. Recovering a prescribed count requires
a local coefficient estimate, beyond concentration of the random count. Theorem 14.4 supplies
the fixed-count extension: tight volume estimates and a positive Fourier bound allow coefficient
extraction at every cusp density. Corollary 14.5 then treats every subcritical cusp weight, including
arbitrary approach to the critical endpoint. The planar separating curves in Theorem 14.1 instead
become contractible after filling the cusps, and lie outside the tight spectrum. In genus zero, Budd
and Curien showed that the Weil–Petersson random sphere with n + 1 cusps, with its metric rescaled
by n−1/4 , converges to the Brownian sphere [12].
1.8.4. Zograf ’s constant and contemporaneous work. The first version of this paper was made public
on Zenodo on 16 October 2026 [53], at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23414478. All numbered
results and sections cited in this paragraph refer
                                               √ to Zenodo v1, not to the present numbering. That
version already contained the constant C = 1/ π (Corollary 1.2 and Section 7), the uniform volume-
ratio limits (Theorem 1.1), the diagonal chain (Theorem 1.3), the adjacent-coefficient asymptotics
over all degrees with the rate O(g −1/8 ) (Theorems 1.4 and 17.1), the absolute expansion uniform in
the cusp count with growing-order remainder bounds (Theorems 14.1–14.2), the vanishing spectral
gap and many small eigenvalues (Corollaries 1.5–1.6), and the short-curve processes and the systole
(Theorem 19.1).
   In contemporaneous work, √  Liu and Xu [56], whose preprint was submitted to arXiv on 24 October
2026, also determine C = 1/ π. Their deformation joins the cubic curve y = z − 4z 3 to the sine
14                                     ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


curve y = sin(2πz)/(2π) while keeping the first positive zero fixed. For each fixed number n ≥ 2 of
markings, their uniform-variation theorem [56, Theorem 1.1] controls the logarithmic derivative of
the normalized volumes uniformly in the curve parameter t ∈ [0, 1] by On (g −1 ). Transport from the
cubic endpoint determines the constant [56, Corollary 1.2], and their first-correction theorem [56,
Theorem 1.3] adds the 1/g term with an On (g −2 ) remainder, uniformly in t.
   Our deformation is instead the string flow associated with the cusp weight x = X(Y ). Its
                                                                                               (p)
amplitudes with a fixed number p of distinguished markings encode the derivatives Φg (x) of the
cusp generating function. Both arguments use positivity, descendant recursions, the dilaton and
KdV equations, and a Painlevé I normalization, but along different deformation families. In the
present paper, Section 7 already determines the constant by specializing to p = 1 and Y = 0, hence
to Vg,1 : uniformity in the total cusp count is not needed for this identification. Its role is the stronger
conclusion of an expansion valid for every n ≥ 0, including n growing with g. This requires the
coefficient extraction in Sections 9–10 and Appendix D of the present paper, beyond the estimates
for a fixed number of distinguished markings.
1.9. Organization and notation. Organization. The main text proves the volume-ratio and
adjacent-coefficient limits and the leading-order estimates. It states the higher-order expansions and
the tight and critical spectrum theorems with outlines of their proofs; the supporting arguments and
the proofs of these statements are collected in Appendices A–G. The paper has four parts.
• The positive recursion (Sections 2–5). Section 2 introduces the kernels, Section 3 the density
   parameter, Section 4 the shifted amplitudes and their positive recursion, and Section 5 compares
   the recursion with the harmonic model. The special-function facts and the bounds for pure ψ
   intersections are proved in Appendices A and B.
• Generating functions (Sections 6–8). Section 6 proves the cusp and genus ratios for a fixed number
   of markings, Section 7 the leading term and Zograf’s constant, and Section 8 states the higher-order
   expansion, which is proved in Appendix C.
• Individual volumes (Sections 9–11). Section 9 proves the local limit theorem and the volume-ratio
   limits, Section 10 states the sharper extraction theorems proved in Appendix D, and Section 11
   proves Theorem 1.1 and the rate in Theorem 1.3.
• Applications (Sections 12–14). Section 12 treats the diagonal chain, Section 13 the adjacent
   coefficients, with the rate proved in Appendix E, and Section 14 the geometry of random surfaces,
   with the proofs of the tight and critical spectrum theorems in Appendices F and G.
The final section summarizes the picture, explains the matrix-model meaning of the parameter and
lists open questions.
    Proof guide. The following table lists where the main results are proved and the principal results
and methods used in their proofs. References to Sections 2–5 include the supporting Appendices A
and B.

   The complexity parameter h is specified locally: we use h = 2g − 2 + n for volumes with n
boundary components and h = 2g − 2 + p for generating functions with p distinguished markings.
In particular, h = 2g − 1 in (1.5), where p = 1 and the remaining cusps are counted by k. Positive
constants C, c may change from line to line, and a subscript, as in Op , indicates the parameters on
which an implied constant depends. A few letters have an additional local meaning, stated where it
occurs: D is the degree sum |d| in Sections 4 and 7 and in Appendix C; h0 = 2g − 2 is the complexity
of a closed surface (Appendices C, F and G); the harmonic coefficients Am (h) are unrelated to the
kernel scalar A(Y ) and to the coefficients Aj (g, n); the Laplacian ∆X of a surface X is unrelated to
∆(Y ); and T is a length window in Section 14.

                                          2. Positive kernels
   Two entire functions of a variable z, depending on the parameter Y , control everything that follows.
The first, KY , arises in Section 4 when the marking singled out by the Dijkgraaf–Verlinde–Verlinde
equation is merged with the κ1 insertions: these mergers act on the amplitudes as KY (E), where E
raises a degree by one, so the Taylor coefficients of 1/KY become the coefficients of the recursion.
The second, HY , plays the same role for the dilaton equation and for the explicit comparison model.
The later arguments need three properties: all zeros of KY are positive and simple (Lemma 2.1);
1/KY and 1/HY have nonnegative Taylor coefficients, even as functions of Y (Lemma 2.2); and
after rescaling by the first zero of KY both kernels form a compact family, including the endpoint
Y = y0 (Subsection 2.2). The last property is what makes all later constants uniform in Y . The first
two are special-function statements; their proofs are given in Appendix A.
   Recall from Subsection 1.1 that

2.1. Real zeros and coefficient positivity.
Lemma 2.1 (Real zeros). On [0, π 2 /4], X(y) > 0 for y > 0, X ′′ < 0, and X ′′′ > 0. Moreover
y0 < 3/2; in particular X(Y ) ≤ Y < 3/2 for 0 ≤ Y ≤ y0 . All zeros of a(y) are positive real; in
particular a has no zeros in |y| < y0 . For 0 ≤ Y < y0 , every zero of KY is positive and simple, and
                                  Y
(2.3)             KY (z) = a(Y )     (1 − z/rj (Y )),    0 < r1 (Y ) < r2 (Y ) < · · · .
                                   j≥1
                            UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                   17
                                 √     √
   At Y = 0 one has K0 (z) = sin 2z/ 2z, and the lemma is the classical description of the zeros
of the sine function. For Y > 0, integration along the characteristics of a first-order equation for
KY writes KY as sin t minus a small positive correction; concavity then locates the real zeros, and
Rouché’s theorem shows that there are no others.
   Reality of the zeros of KY does not imply it for HY ; indeed some higher zeros of HY can be
nonreal. The required substitute is the following stronger coefficient assertion.
Lemma 2.2 (Coefficient positivity). For 0 ≤ Y < y0 ,
             a(Y )      X
(2.4)    log         =      tm (Y )z m ,   t(q)
                                            m (0) ≥ 0          (m ≥ 1, q ≥ 0),   tm (0) > 0,
             HY (z)
                           m≥1

where each tm is analytic on |Y | < y0 and its Taylor series at zero converges to tm (Y ) there.
Consequently HY−h has strictly positive Taylor coefficients for every real h > 0, and −HY′ /HY has
nonnegative coefficients. Moreover every Taylor coefficient in Y of
                                    ϑbℓ (Y ) := a(Y )[z ℓ ]KY (z)−1
is nonnegative, and the same holds for a−1 and for ϑℓ = [z ℓ ]KY−1 .
   The coefficients tm satisfy a system of ordinary differential equations in Y whose right-hand sides
are polynomials with nonnegative coefficients, and they are positive at Y = 0, where H0 has only
positive real zeros; induction on the order of differentiation gives the lemma. Note that these are
statements about Taylor coefficients in the parameter Y , which is stronger than positivity at each
real Y ; they supply the positive convolution factor of Section 9.1.
2.2. Rescaled kernels and a local limit theorem. Set r = r1 (Y ),
(2.5)       K̄Y (w) = KY (rw)/a(Y ),      H̄Y (w) = HY (rw)/a(Y ),         A(Y ) = H̄Y (1)/3.
The first zero r is simple and varies analytically. Integration of (2.2) gives
                                                   Z z

                                            2z      0
so HY (z) > 0 on [0, r]. Since HY−1 has nonnegative Taylor coefficients, its radius of convergence
exceeds r. Indeed, by Pringsheim’s theorem a power series with nonnegative coefficients is singular
at the positive point of its circle of convergence; if the radius were at most r, that point would be a
zero of HY in (0, r]. In particular HY has no complex zero of modulus at most r.
   At the critical endpoint put b = −X ′′ (y0 ) > 0. The equation KY (r) = 0 and Taylor expansion at
Y = y0 , r = 0 give
(2.6)            r(Y ) ∼ 3a(Y )/b,     K̄Y (w) −→ 1 − w,          H̄Y (w) −→ 1 − 3w/5.
These convergences are locally uniform in complex w. The family therefore extends continuously to
Y = y0 . Compactness and nonvanishing on the closed unit disk give one η > 0 such that all H̄Y−1
are analytic and uniformly bounded on |w| ≤ 1 + η, including this endpoint. Likewise K̄Y /(1 − w) is
nonvanishing on a common disk of radius greater than one: its zeros are the higher positive roots

Indeed, on a fixed neighborhood of zero the centered characteristic logarithm is −σY2 t2 /2 + O(|t|3 ),
uniformly by the exponential moments. Away from zero its modulus is uniformly less than one,
because the first two probabilities have uniform positive lower bounds. Scaling t by h−1/2 , these
facts give an integrable Gaussian bound and pointwise convergence; Fourier inversion proves (2.9).
At a bounded displacement from the mean the error is relative O(h−1 ), by expansion through fourth
order and cancellation of the odd cubic integral. Thus

has a uniform central lower bound cL H̄Y (1)−h / h, and its logarithm at a bounded displacement is
−h log H̄Y (1) − 12 log h + O(1). These are the coefficient estimates used below.

                                    3. The density parameter
    This section establishes the properties of the functions T , ∆ = T − Y , I and λ defined in (1.2) and
(1.3). Their meaning is as follows. The first positive zero of KY is r(Y ) = 2∆(Y ); equation (1.2) is
the condition KY (2∆) = 0 written in the variable v = T − ∆s2 . The integral I(Y ) is the action that
governs the exponential growth in (1.4), and λ(Y )/2 is the logarithmic derivative of I(Y )−1 with
respect to log x, that is, the leading cusp-count scale per unit complexity. Lemma 3.1 below shows
that T is well defined and that λ is an analytic increasing bijection from [0, y0 ) onto [0, ∞), so that
the profiles B, C of (1.7) are functions of the density λ ≥ 0; it also records the behavior of all four
functions at the two ends of the interval. Its proof, which uses the Sonine integral and elementary
calculus, is given in Appendix A.3.
    For the local-limit arguments we view λ as a function of the cusp weight and put

                   4. Intersection amplitudes and the comparison model
  We deform the Weil–Petersson intersection brackets into pure ψ intersection numbers and derive
a positive recursion along the deformation. We call the resulting functions shifted amplitudes. The
harmonic model introduced below is an explicit, solvable comparison for this recursion. Section 5
compares the full recursion with that model, uniformly in the degrees and in the deformation
parameter. This comparison is the common starting point for the volume and coefficient asymptotics.
4.1. Intersection identities and normalization. We use the following notation for a stable pair
(g, n) and a degree tuple d: h is the complexity, N the dimension of Mg,n , and the deficit m is the
number of κ1 factors needed to fill up the dimension. Write
                                                             Xn
               h = 2g − 2 + n,      N = 3g − 3 + n,     D=      di ,   m = N − D.
                                                                    i=1
Throughout the comparison theorem n ≥ 1, the pair is stable, and 0 ≤ D ≤ N . A tuple with a
negative degree or an unstable pair has amplitude zero.
   Recall X, a = X ′ > 0 on [0, y0 ) and the kernels KY and HY of (2.1), and write rY = r(Y ) for the
first positive zero of KY . The properties of these kernels used below were established in Section 2.

4.3. The harmonic model. We now introduce the explicit model with which the amplitudes will
be compared. It depends on the degrees only through the deficit m, and it is chosen so that the
linear part of (4.8) reproduces it exactly on average; this is the mass identity (4.12) below, and the
reason for the name: the model is harmonic for the linear part of the recursion. At the endpoint
Y = y0 the model is the denominator in the normalization (4.3) of pure ψ intersection numbers
(see (7.14)), so the comparison theorem extends the coarse bounds on pure ψ intersections to the
whole parameter interval.

children. We check that these weights have total mass one when the pivot degree d1 is positive; this
is the meaning of (4.12). Assume g ≥ 2; states of genus at most one are terminal in the argument of
Section 5. A merging child has type (g, n − 1) and a nonseparating child type (g − 1, n + 1); both
have complexity h − 1 and deficit m − ℓ. Hence the model ratio of a merging child to its parent is
Am−ℓ (h − 1)/(3(h − 1)Am (h)), and that of a nonseparating child is four times larger, because of the
factor 4−g in (4.11). The merging children carry the coefficients (2dj + 1)θℓ , j ≥ 2, and there are
d1 + ℓ − 1 ordered nonseparating pairs, each with coefficient θℓ /2. Since 2D + n = 3h − 2m, the
total weight is
                m    P                                 m

by (4.12). Thus, for a positive pivot, the linear model weights form a probability kernel P , and
positivity of the separating terms gives R ≥ P R.

                           5. Comparison with the harmonic model
Proposition 5.1 (Uniform harmonic comparison). For every fixed n ≥ 1, there is a constant Cn
such that
                                                  Ug,n (d; Y )         √
                      −Cn log2 (h + 1) ≤ log h     −g   m       −h
                                                                   ≤ Cn h
                                            3 Γ(h)4 [z ]H̄Y (z)
                                          n
for every stable pair (g, n), every d ∈ Z≥0 with |d| ≤ 3g − 3 + n, and every Y ∈ [0, y0 ]. Here
h = 2g − 2 + n and m = 3g − 3 + n − |d|.
   The idea is the following. Write R = U/M for the ratio of a true amplitude to the model
amplitude (4.11), and divide the recursion (4.8) by the model. Because the model satisfies the linear
part of the recursion with total mass one (identity (4.12)), the linear terms express R at a state as
an average of R over the states of complexity h − 1 reached in one step, and the separating terms
add a nonnegative amount. Thus R is bounded below by its expectation along a random walk that
lowers the complexity, and it is bounded above as soon as the separating contribution is small.
   The comparison of intersection recursions with random walks has an important precedent in
Aggarwal’s proof of uniform asymptotics for pure ψ intersections [2]. Here the recursion and its
comparison model vary with Y ; the estimates must also remain uniform as this parameter approaches
the pure-ψ endpoint.
   Throughout this section a state is a stable pair (g, n), n ≥ 1, with a degree tuple d ∈ Zn≥0 ,
|d| ≤ 3g − 3 + n, and a parameter Y ∈ [0, y0 ]; constants are uniform in Y and in the state unless
stated otherwise. We write Z0 for the number of entries of d equal to zero. A state is terminal if
m = 0 or g ≤ 1; the random walks below stop there. The proof has three steps.
• Lemma 5.2 bounds R at terminal states, using only the string and dilaton equations and the
  coarse bounds of Appendix B.
• Lemma 5.3 proves the lower bound. The only defect of the averaging is at entries of degree zero,
  where some linear terms are missing; we show that the random walk does not create many such
  entries.
• Lemmas 5.4–5.6 prove the upper bound. We divide R by a weight that penalizes entries of degree
  zero and one, show that the weighted separating terms have relative size O(h−1/2 ), and conclude
  by examining, at each complexity, the state at which the weighted ratio is largest.
5.1. Bounds at terminal states.
Lemma 5.2 (Terminal bounds). There are constants cψ , Cψ , c, C > 0 with the following properties.
   (i) For every pure ψ tuple e with Z0 zero entries, cψ (2/3)Z0 ≤ Cg (e) ≤ Cψ 2Z0 , in the normalization
       (4.3).
  (ii) At every terminal state, R ≥ c e−CZ0 .

Proof. For (i), the standard normalized string and dilaton identities imply
(5.1)                                cψ (2/3)Z0 ≤ Cg (e) ≤ Cψ 2Z0 ,

zero, let p+ denote the number of positive entries; normalized string has total positive coefficient
                                        2(3g − 3 + q) + p+
                                           3(2g − 3 + q)
between 2/3 and 2. Once the ones have been removed, each string child has one fewer zero; any
newly created one can again be removed. With no zeros use the primitive bounds stated after (4.3);
the remaining bases are C0 (0, 0, 0) = 1/3 and C1 (1) = 1/6. Induction proves (5.1), which is (i).

5.2. The lower bound: control of zero degrees.
Lemma 5.3 (Lower bound). There are constants c, C > 0 such that, at every state,
                            R ≥ c exp{−C(Z0 + log(h + 1)) log(h + 1)}.
Proof. Since the separating terms of (4.8) are nonnegative, R is at least the average of R over its
linear children. Iterating, R is bounded below by the expected terminal value along a random walk
that lowers the complexity by one at each step. Two losses occur: the terminal bounds (5.1) and
(5.3) lose a factor exponential in the number of zero entries, and the averaging may have mass s < 1
at states with all degrees zero, again with a loss controlled by the number of zeros. It therefore
suffices to show that the walk rarely creates many zero entries.
   At a nonterminal state choose any positive pivot if one exists; otherwise choose a zero. Stop only
at m = 0 or g ≤ 1. Use the linear model weights, divided by their mass s, as transition probabilities.
For a positive pivot s = 1; at an all-zero state use (4.13)–(4.14).
26                                   ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


   Let Z be the current zero count. At a positive pivot, a merger with a marking of degree zero
lowers Z by one, except when the pivot has degree one and ℓ = 0; that merger leaves Z unchanged.
A nonseparating ordered pair can increase Z by at most two, and at each ℓ there are at most two
pairs containing a zero. Each pair has twice the model weight of a merger with one zero marking.
Thus
(5.5)                              pbirth ≤ 4pdeath /Z      (Z ≥ 1).
That exceptional term contributes neither a birth nor a death. At an all-zero state, a birth occurs
only at ℓ = 2, raises the count by one, and its weight is at most 2/(Z − 1) times the mass of mergers
with a zero marking. Those mergers have ℓ ≥ 1 and lower the count by at least one. Hence (5.5)
still holds for Z ≥ 2. Normalization by s preserves these comparisons.
   For Z ≥ 24, the conditional drift obeys
                                 E(2Znext )                1
                                      Z
                                            − 1 ≤ 3pbirth − pdeath ≤ 0.
                                    2                      2
During each excursion above 24, optional stopping bounds the probability of reaching M by 2Zstart −M .
An excursion entered from below starts at a value at most 26. Since complexity decreases by one,
there are at most hinitial + 1 excursions. A union bound therefore shows that, with probability at
least 1/2,
(5.6)                        max Z ≤ Zinitial + C + C log(hinitial + 1).
All stopping times are bounded, so optional stopping applies without further integrability conditions.
  Only all-zero steps can have s < 1. By (4.13)–(4.14),
                                        − log min(s, 1) ≤ CZ/h.

5.3. The upper bound: a weight for degrees zero and one. For the upper bound the separating
terms must be shown to be small, and for general states they are not: a state with many entries of
degree zero or one can distribute these markings between the two components in many ways. We
therefore divide R by a weight that grows with the number of such entries. It is chosen so that
the linear terms still average R/W up to a factor 1 + O(h−1/2 ) (this is (5.9)), while the weighted
separating terms become small (Subsection 5.4).
   Call degrees zero or one inactive. Let ι be their number, Z0 the number of zeros, and Ract the
number of degrees at least two. Fix a sufficiently small constant c∗ > 0, and set
                                  Yι           p
(5.8)                      ϕ(ι) =    max(1, c∗ j),       W(d) = 4Z0 ϕ(ι).
                                  j=1
                             UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                    27


We call the truncation max(1, ·) clipping; it makes ϕ nondecreasing,
                                                           √               since every factor is at least
one. Up to fixed multiplicative constants, ϕ(ι) equals cι∗ ι!. Its monotonicity is essential when an
inactive pivot splits into two active degrees.
   In the upper bound we choose the pivot as follows: an active entry when one exists, an entry
equal to one if no active entry exists, and a zero only at an all-zero state. Again the walk stops only
at terminal states. The linear model weights pchild of a state are the weights that appear when (4.8)
is divided by the model; their mass is given by (4.12)–(4.14), and may exceed one at an all-zero
state with one or two markings.
Lemma 5.4 (Weighted linear terms). If c∗ is sufficiently small, then at every nonterminal state
                       X           Wchild
(5.9)                       pchild        ≤ 1 + CS0 /h ≤ 1 + Ch−1/2 .
                                    W
                             linear

Proof. The second inequality is (4.21). We consider the three possible pivots in turn.
   For an active pivot, an ordinary inactive merger reduces ι by at least one and does not increase
Z0 . For all sufficiently large ι, its weight ratio is at most 1/2. The exception is a pivot of degree two
merged with a zero at ℓ = 0: it turns that zero into a one, leaves ι fixed, and divides the weight by
four. The favorable mass is therefore at least a fixed positive multiple of ιS0 /h.
  At each ℓ, at most four nonseparating pairs contain an inactive degree. A pair creating two
inactive degrees has weight ratio, for large ι, at most
                                                 p
                                            16c2∗ (ι + 1)(ι + 2).
Its total model mass per ℓ is bounded by a fixed multiple of qℓ /h. Choose c∗ so small that the
coefficient of ι in this upper bound
                                   √ is less than half the favorable merger coefficient. Pairs creating
only one inactive degree cost O( ι), and all other pairs leave the weight unchanged. The remaining
expression is bounded above by CS0 /h. Bounded ι, including the clipping threshold, also costs only
CS0 /h, because there are at most four exceptional pairs and boundedly many inactive mergers.
   If the pivot has degree one and all markings are inactive, every merger decreases ι, while a
nonseparating step increases it by at most one. Only four pairs per ℓ can increase the weight; a pair
with two active new degrees decreases ι and cannot increase the clipped weight. The same argument
applies, with ι − 1 favorable merging labels.
   At an all-zero state, all valid mergers have ℓ ≥ 1 and decrease both the zero count and the inactive
weight. Increasing pairs have ℓ ≥ 2 and increase ι by at most one. Use S0 − q0 in both birth and
merger estimates. The unweighted mass correction (4.13) is nonpositive for n ≥ 3 and O(h−1 )
otherwise. This proves (5.9) in every case.                                                          □

5.6. Completion of the comparison.
Completion of the proof of Proposition 5.1. For fixed n, the zero count and the inactive count are

                         6. Ratios of marked generating functions
   The comparison theorem controls logarithms, whereas the volume-ratio problem requires relative
errors tending to zero. We obtain that stronger control here for generating functions with a fixed
number of distinguished markings. The two ratios correspond to adding a cusp and to replacing one
unit of genus by two cusps. Their errors are O(1/g), uniformly in the parameter. The passage to
volumes with a prescribed cusp count will be made in Section 9.
   The mechanism is simple. Positivity of the recursion implies that an amplitude decreases when
one of its degrees is raised (6.7). The dilaton equation (6.4) writes the cusp ratio as a weighted sum,
over degrees d ≥ 1, of the normalized amplitudes cg,p+1 (d, 0p ). These weights can have either sign;
                              UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                     31


their absolute moments are uniformly bounded by (6.5). The KdV equation (6.6) writes the genus
ratio through cg,p (1, 0p−1 ) and separating terms. Both ratios are therefore within O(1/g) of explicit
functions of Y once we know that cg,p (d) = 1 − O(|d|2 /g). This last estimate, (6.15), follows from
the difference of the recursion at adjacent degrees; the coarse bounds of Section 5 are used only to
show that the separating terms are small.
6.1. Shifted amplitudes and normalization. Use p for the number of distinguished markings,
and keep n for the number of cusps. Retain the kernels and shifted amplitudes of Sections 2
                             Y (d), and suppress Y when convenient. Put h = 2g − 2 + p and

   We now show that the endpoint value persists on a short interval 0 ≤ y0 − Y ≤ h−2 . The reason
is that the amplitudes are polynomials with nonnegative coefficients in the kernel coefficients, and
these are within a factor 1 + O(y0 − Y ) of their endpoint value one. Here θℓ = [z ℓ ]K̄Y (z)−1 are the
rescaled kernel coefficients. Put δ = y0 − Y . The normalized kernels are analytic in δ near zero on
a common disk of radius greater than one. To see the removable parameter singularity, apply the
implicit-function theorem to Ky0 −δ (δρ)/δ at (δ, ρ) = (0, 3); its ρ derivative is nonzero. Division by
the persistent root 1 − z then gives K̄Y (z)/(1 − z) = 1 + O(δ) on a smaller common disk. Together
with coefficient positivity this proves
(7.15)                              1 ≤ θℓ (Y ) ≤ 1 + Cδ         for all ℓ ≥ 0.

             8. Higher-order corrections for marked generating functions
   The estimate of Proposition 7.1 has relative error Op (log g/g). The logarithm comes from
integrating the error Op (1/(hAr)) in (7.11) towards the critical endpoint, where Ar has a simple
zero. This section states two refinements, still for a fixed number p of distinguished markings: an
explicit first correction, which also gives the leading term with relative error Op (g −1 ), and a complete
                                                       √
expansion whose truncation order may grow like g. Both results are proved in Appendix C; the
passage to a prescribed cusp count is made in Sections 9 and 10.
8.1. The first correction. The mechanism is that of Section 7, carried one order further. Lemma C.1
extends the degree expansion (7.5) of the normalized profiles by a term ep (d)/h2 , uniformly for
|d| ≤ h1/8 . Inserted into the dilaton identity, it adds a term γp /h to the numerator of the differential
equation (7.8); see (C.8). The coefficient γp , defined in (C.7), is a finite expression in kernel moments
(see (C.11)), and it vanishes at the pure-ψ endpoint (see (C.12)). Its primitive against 1/(Ar) is
therefore bounded, although Ar vanishes there. The integration constant is fixed at the endpoint by
the first correction to Cg (23g−3 ), which we derive from Painlevé I in Subsection C.9, together with
the first correction to an explicit negative-binomial coefficient. Accordingly, define
                                                                    Z y0
                            −2p2 + 12p − 11                                γp (v)
(8.1)                 tp =                   ,       Lp (Y ) = tp −                dv.
                                   20                                Y A(v)r(v)
Theorem 8.1 (Uniform marked first correction). For every fixed p ≥ 1, uniformly for 0 ≤ Y < y0 ,
                                                                         
                                Jg,p (Y )       Lp (Y )         log(g + 1)
(8.2)            Φ(p)
                   g  (X(Y )) =             1 +         + O p                 .

In particular the leading relative error is Op (g −1 ).
  At Y = 0 the coefficients of this expansion reduce to the first-order formulas of Mirzakhani and
Zograf [66, Theorems 1.4 and 3.2(i)]; this check is recorded at the end of Subsection C.1.
                              UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                  39


8.2. All orders. The same scheme continues to every order. The recursion is triangular: each new
degree coefficient of the profiles uses earlier cusp and genus coefficients, and the dilaton and KdV
equations then give the new cusp and genus coefficients. The difficulty is to keep the constants under
control as the order grows.
    Where the squared factorial comes from. The first two profile coefficients are polynomials in the
degrees of degrees two and four: δp in (7.5) is quadratic in D = |d|, and ep in (C.3) is quartic. This
pattern persists. Each new order is obtained by summing an adjacent difference along a coordinate
path of length D, and each difference involves pair counts of size D and one factor 1/h. So the
order-j coefficient behaves like a polynomial of degree 2j in the degrees, of size about (D + j)2j up
to a factor B j . In the dilaton sum the degree d is weighted by the geometrically decaying coefficients
bd , so the effective size of D is of order j, and (M j)2j is (j!)2 up to a geometric factor. The
                                                                                               √ bound
B K+1              2
        ((K + 1)!) h −K−1   for the first√omitted term is smallest at an order proportional to h, and a
sufficiently small fixed multiple of h gives an exponentially small remainder. This explains both
the Gevrey-2 bounds and the truncation order in Theorem 8.2.
    Appendix C constructs, by the finite triangular rules (C.13)–(C.18), the formal coefficients of the
profiles and of the cusp and genus ratios, and from them functions ap,j (Y ) and
                                                 j
                                                X            
                           Lp,j (Y ) = [tj ] exp   ap,i (Y )ti ,   Lp,1 = Lp ;
                                                i=1
see (C.38). On one complex neighborhood of [0, y0 ] they are holomorphic and satisfy ∥ap,j ∥+∥Lp,j ∥ ≤
Cp Bjp (j!)2 .
Theorem 8.2 (Complete uniform marked expansion).√For each fixed p ≥ 1 there are Cp , Bp , cp , hp >
0 such that, for h = 2g − 2 + p ≥ hp and 0 ≤ K ≤ cp h,
                        (p)             K
                    πΦg (X(Y )) X Lp,j (Y )   Cp BK+1

(8.3)         sup               −           ≤                     ,               Lp,0 = 1.
            0≤Y <y0   Jg,p (Y )     hj              hK+1
                                       j=0

The coefficients are specified by the finite rules and integrals of Appendix C, and have the Gevrey-
2 norms stated above on one complex neighborhood of [0, y0 ]. The same real error holds √for the
logarithm with coefficients ap,j . A sufficiently small square-root truncation has error Op (e−ap h ); no
convergence of the infinite series is required.
   The proof has three steps. Proposition C.3 divides each order-j coefficient by (D + p + M j)2j .
After this division the coefficient rules become coefficientwise inequalities between power series with
nonnegative coefficients, and a system of analytic majorants (Lemma C.2) bounds the normalized
coefficients and the actual remainders, uniformly in the order. Next, at the pure-ψ endpoint, the
integration constants are computed from the Itzykson–Zuber identity and the large-order behavior
of Painlevé I; the Borel representation of Costin, Costin and Huang [18] gives these constants with
a Gevrey-1 remainder. Finally the logarithmic derivative is integrated from a matching point at
distance h−K−2 from the endpoint, where the positive homogeneous structure of the amplitudes
controls the change from the endpoint value.
   At Y = 0 this is the established fixed-cusp expansion mechanism; effective order-dependent
versions appear in Hide–Macera–Thomas [38, Theorem 4.2]. The estimates here remain uniform up
to the pure-ψ endpoint. Their dependence on the order is what permits the growing-order truncation
in the absolute-volume theorem.

                              9. Extracting a prescribed cusp count
   The preceding estimates control generating functions, which sum over cusp counts. We now
recover a single coefficient, and hence a volume with a prescribed number of cusps. A positive
40                                     ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


convolution factor supplies Fourier bounds that turn the real-axis estimates into a local limit theorem.
Small cusp weight, the interior, and the critical endpoint require separate estimates, with uniform
constants across the three ranges.
   Subsection 9.4 proves the qualitative volume-ratio limits using the leading estimates from Sections 6–
7. The final subsection extends the argument to bounded geodesic boundaries. The higher-order
expansions are needed only for the sharper extraction estimates that follow.
9.1. A positive convolution factor.
Lemma 9.1 (Positive convolution factor). For each stable marked pair (g, p) with p ≥ 1, the marked
generating function has the exact factorization
(9.1)      Φ(p)          h
            g (x) = V (x) Rg,p (x),        V (x) = Y ′ (x) = 1/a(Y (x)),         h = 2g − 2 + p,
where Rg,p has nonnegative Taylor coefficients and converges for 0 ≤ x < xc .
                                                                     (p)
   The point of (9.1) is the following. An approximation of Φg on the real axis alone says nothing
about its individual coefficients. The factorization shows that, under the cusp weight x, the number
of additional cusps is distributed as the sum of h independent variables with generating function
V (xz)/V (x) and one further independent nonnegative integer variable; this gives the Fourier bound
(9.2) below, which is what a local limit theorem needs.
Proof. The proof uses positivity in the parameter: by Lemma 2.2, the functions ϑbℓ (Y ) =
a(Y )[z ℓ ]KY (z)−1 and 1/a(Y ) have nonnegative Taylor coefficients in Y . Since this positivity
in Y is available for the unrescaled coefficients, we do not use the rescaling by r(Y ) of (4.6) here.
   Undo the normalization in (4.8) and regard the unrescaled coefficients ϑℓ = [z ℓ ]KY (z)−1 as
independent variables. In this convention the bases are U0,3 = ϑ0 , U1,1 (1) = ϑ0 /8, and U1,1 (0) = ϑ1 /8.
Induction from these bases shows that every amplitude is a finite polynomial with nonnegative
coefficients in the variables ϑℓ ; each monomial has total degree h, and weighted degree m when
ϑℓ is given weight ℓ. A linear recursion term adds one factor ϑℓ to a child of complexity h − 1; a
separating term adds one to children whose complexities sum to h − 1. Thus factoring a−1 from
each of the h factors ϑℓ = a−1 ϑbℓ leaves a positive polynomial in the ϑbℓ .
   Finally Y (x) has nonnegative coefficients and Y (0) = 0. To justify the product used here, a
                                          √

9.4. Ratios at a prescribed number of cusps. We now prove the limits in Theorem 1.3. Each
volume ratio is a ratio of coefficients, at the same index, of two marked generating functions. We

model center of both. The local limit theorem (9.7) shows that the two local probabilities are
asymptotically equal, so the coefficient ratio is asymptotic to the ratio of the generating functions
themselves, which Section 6 determines.

slope use the endpoint equivalents (3.4) of Lemma 3.1. Since λeff /(n/g) → 1 whenever n → ∞,
these give the desired relative replacement in all cases.
   Finally the exact identity
(9.22)                                        Dg,n = Cg,n−1 /Cg,n
and the adjacent continuity of the profile C give Dg,n → 1 for every growing-cusp sequence. For
bounded cusp counts, the classical fixed-cusp theorems give Bg,n → 1, Cg,n → 4π 2 , and hence
Dg,n → 1; these are the only bounded-cusp inputs used here.
  If any supremum in Theorem 1.3 did not tend to zero, select a violating sequence of cusp counts.
A subsequence either has a fixed bounded cusp count or tends to infinity. The preceding conclusions
contradict a fixed violation in both cases. This proves the uniform convergence assertions. Section 11.1
supplies the stated inverse-genus rate using the sharper extraction theorem.
                                                                                                      □

                       10. Extraction with an inverse-genus error
   The local limit theorem (9.7) has no rate. Its quantitative form, Proposition D.1 in Appendix D,
gives only a power rate in min(h, µ), and its Gaussian approximation degenerates when the coefficient
index stays bounded. To obtain an error of order g −1 uniformly in the index, we retain an exact
Poisson factor rather than its Gaussian approximation. Theorem 10.1 supplies the estimate used for
the leading absolute-volume formula in Section 11, and Theorem 10.2 extends it to all orders. Both
theorems are proved in Appendix D.
                                                                         (p)
   Why a Poisson factor. Near zero cusp weight the leading term of Φg behaves like ehS(x) with
S(x) = 2x + O(x2 ); see (10.1) below. At a positive weight x, the normalized terms e−2hx (2hx)k /k!
are exactly Poisson probabilities with mean 2hx. Hence, when the coefficient index k stays bounded,
the cusp count under the saddle weight is approximately Poisson, and a Gaussian approximation
of its point mass makes a relative error of order 1/k, which does not tend to zero. If the leading
term is written instead with the exact Poisson mass pk = e−k k k /k! at the mean,
                                                                               √     the ratio of the
true coefficient to this model is 1 + O(1/h) for every k. For large k one has 2πk pk → 1, and the
Gaussian formula is recovered.
                            (p)
   Fix p ≥ 1, put Fh = Φg , h = 2g − 2 + p, and write
                                                                                     √
                                 hS(x)                   Z(0)                    rp/2 AΘ
(10.1)      Jg,p (x) = Jg,p (0)e       Bp (x), S = log          ,    Bp =          p           .
                                                       Z(Y (x))             r(0)p/2 A(0)Θ(0)

                                             s
