At k = 0 take x = 0 and interpret both k-dependent factors by continuity as one.
   The proof, given in Subsections D.2–D.4, has three parts: transfer a sufficiently long marked
expansion, namely Theorem 8.2 at a fixed finite order, to small complex neighborhoods of the real
saddle point; identify the Poisson correction; and estimate the small indices directly. The argument
uses no estimate for a growing number of markings and never differentiates a remainder; away from
zero, the model is continued analytically only to small neighborhoods of the real axis.
10.1. The full expansion at a prescribed cusp count. Theorem 10.1 recovers the leading term
with an inverse-genus error. We now extract the whole marked expansion, keeping the remainder
uniform in both the cusp count and the truncation order. The result is Theorem 10.2, which provides
the remaining input for the full absolute-volume expansion in Section 11.
   The idea. Away from zero cusp weight, including near the critical point, the coefficient integral
has a nondegenerate saddle point, and the Gaussian method of steepest descent gives an expansion
in 1/h. Near zero this fails for bounded k: the saddle point of ehS(z) z −k approaches the singularity
of z −k at the origin. Following Temme, we change variables so that the phase S(z) − (k/h) log z
becomes exactly u − (k/h) log u. The coefficient is then a Poisson coefficient [uk ]ehu f (u) with an
analytic amplitude f . Expanding f about u = k/h produces the signed central polynomials Tm (k)
of the coefficient operator in (D.19), divided by powers of h. These are not the central moments of a
probability distribution: for example, T2 (k) = −k. The resulting expansion in 1/h is uniform in k,
including k = 0. The two expansions are identified on an overlap of their ranges.
   The proof, given in Subsections D.5–D.7, uses a Poisson integral near zero cusp weight and a
Gaussian integral away from zero, including near criticality. A change of phase identifies the two
coefficient formulas on their overlap. Near zero we follow Temme’s method for uniform Laplace and
loop integrals [80, Sections 2.3 and 3.7].
   Retain h = 2g − 2 + p, k = hΨ(x), v = DΨ(x) and the functions S, Bp in (10.1). Regard Lp,j as
functions of x via Y (x). Write

                                    12. The full diagonal chain
   The absolute expansion assumes g → ∞. To reach the bounded-genus end of the diagonal chain,
we instead sum the logarithms of the cusp ratios of Theorem 1.3 and compare with the fixed-genus
asymptotics of Manin and Zograf. This proves Theorem 1.5, with F as in (1.8). The result has only
logarithmic precision, although the cusp ratios are known with relative errors: summing n of their
logarithms accumulates an error nεg with εg → 0.
   We use the functions T, ∆, I, J and the profile C of Section 3, and the variable t = λ/(2+λ) ∈ [0, 1);
thus Y (u) in (1.8) is the parameter with λ = 2u/(1 − u).

estimates. We first prove the uniform equivalent and then, in Appendix E, quantify its error.

13.5. The lowest degrees. Near degree zero the interior argument gives no information: the

13.6. Combining the degree ranges.
Proof of the uniform equivalent (13.2). Take any sequence of valid indices and a subsequence on
which k/g converges in [0, 3]. An interior limit is covered by (13.17), and a zero limit by (13.26),

violating the supremum statement (13.2) would contradict one of these three cases.              □
  The effective density (2k + 1)/(2g − 1) in (13.1) is essential near the top: it retains the exact
codimension m in 3 − αg,k = 2m/(2g − 1). Replacing it by k/g before taking the endpoint limit
would change the fixed-codimension denominator.

                            14. Short curves and small eigenvalues
   The analytic estimates enter geometry through ratios of volumes of cut surfaces, by Mirzakhani’s
integration formula [61]. We first apply the adjacent-volume limit to Shen–Wu’s vanishing-gap
argument [79] (Subsection 14.1). We then determine the short-curve process and the systole above
          √
the scale g (Subsection 14.2) and deduce many small eigenvalues (Subsection 14.3). The last two
subsections state the limit of the tight length spectrum at every cusp density and of the full primitive
                                      √
spectrum at the critical scale n ≍ g; their proofs are given in Appendices F and G. The critical
result uses volume bounds of Mirzakhani–Zograf and Shen–Wu and only the zero-density case of the
volume-ratio limits, not the uniform expansion.
14.1. The first positive eigenvalue. The simplest spectral consequence uses only the discrete
cusp ratio Dg,n−1 . A short curve enclosing two cusps bounds a pair of pants of fixed area 2π, joined
to the rest of the surface through a short boundary. This gives a small Cheeger ratio and hence a
small Rayleigh quotient. Shen–Wu’s second-moment argument makes such a curve probable. Shen
and Wu asked for the adjacent-volume limit needed to extend this argument beyond n = o(g) [79,
Question 3]. Theorem 1.3 provides that limit along every sequence with g → ∞.
Proof of Corollary 1.6. Put n = n(g) and

                                            Lg =          .

Let Ng (X) count the simple closed geodesics of length at most Lg whose complement has components
of topological types (0, 3) and (g, n − 1). Proposition 35 of [79], in the notation of its arXiv version 4,
                                       √
states under the sole assumption n/ g → ∞ that

                              lim inf PWP
                                       g,n {Ng ≥ 1} ≥ lim inf            .

same conclusion also follows within this paper: Ng is the count NLg of Subsection 14.2, with qg as in
                                         √
(14.1), and since Lg → 0 and qg L2g ≍ n g/(g + n) → ∞, Proposition 14.2 gives Ng /(qg L2g ) → 1 in
probability. Its proof is the same second-moment argument, with Dg,n−1 → 1 as the volume input.
   Write h(X) for the Cheeger constant. On the event Ng ≥ 1, a counted geodesic bounds a hyperbolic
pair of pants of area 2π; the other component has area 2π(2g + n − 3) ≥ 2π for all sufficiently large
g. This separating curve is an admissible Cheeger cut, so
                                                      Lg

                                                      2π
Let R(X) be the infimum of the Rayleigh quotient over nonzero H 1 (X) functions orthogonal to
constants. Buser’s inequality [15, Theorem 7.1], in the form recorded in [79, Theorem 11] for complete
noncompact hyperbolic surfaces, gives R(X) ≤ ch(X) with an absolute constant c. Since Lg → 0,
58                                       ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


this upper bound is eventually smaller than both ε and 1/4. The essential spectrum starts at 1/4;
the min–max principle, as recalled in [79, Theorem 7], therefore yields a positive discrete eigenvalue
λ1 (X) = R(X) < ε on Ng ≥ 1, for all sufficiently large g. This proves the asserted probability
limit.                                                                                              □
  The use of Dg,n−1 above is the only change to the final step of Shen–Wu’s proof: under their
condition n = o(g) this limit follows from [66, Lemma 5.1], as they note. The short-curve estimate,
the Cheeger cut, and the passage to a discrete eigenvalue are their geometric and spectral inputs.

   The method of factorial moments for curves separating off a prescribed number of cusps has
a direct precedent in Hide–Thomas [41, Theorem 1.7] for punctured spheres, which also treats
summed types and nested configurations [41, Remark 1.9 and Proposition 3.6]. Here the uniform
volume ratios give the entire family of type intensities for diverging genus at every cusp density
above the square-root scale. These planar curves become contractible after the cusps are filled;
they are excluded from the tight spectrum of [13, Definition 2.1]. The all-primitive process of
Mirzakhani–Petri [65, Theorem 4.1] concerns closed surfaces at fixed physical lengths. Here the
full process is observed on a shrinking scale; a tight-spectrum limit at its longer natural scale is a
separate question.
   Factorial moments. For sufficiently small L, the collar lemma forces all curves counted by
NL = #{γ ∈ P2 (X) : ℓγ ≤ L} to be disjoint. Their pants interiors are also disjoint. Otherwise one
pants boundary would be an interior essential geodesic of the other pants, hence boundary-parallel,
contradicting uniqueness of geodesic representatives. Thus their pairs of cusp labels are disjoint,
and their complement has type (g, n − r) for r pants. We have g ≥ 2 eventually, so the exceptional
double-pants decomposition of a four-punctured sphere does not arise.
   Mirzakhani’s integration formula [61, 64], with the same conventions as [79, Lemma 29], gives, for
any fixed r and short length windows I1 , . . . , Ir , the ordered distinct-curve integral

Proof of Theorem 14.1. We prove the planar process by bounding the tail over cusp types and
computing the mixed factorial moments for any fixed collection of types. We then exclude the other
topological types on the same length scale; this identifies the full primitive process and the systole.
Write P = 2π 2 , h = 2g − 1, m = n − 1, and Fh = Φ′g . Choose x ∈ (0, xc ) by hΨ(x) = m and
put qg∗ = nx/(4P ). The effective density is 2(n − 1)/(2g − 1). The boundedness of λ∂λ log X(Yλ ),
including its endpoint limits, implies qg∗ /qg → 1 and qg∗ → ∞.

Indeed stability gives av +dv ≥ 3 and v dv = 2r −d, hence a ≥ r +d. The power of x is nonnegative,

configuration is a star. This accounts for every fixed-type mixed factorial moment.
   The limits (14.12) are the mixed factorial moments of independent Poisson counts. Higher fixed
moments give uniform integrability, and the product Poisson law is moment determinate. First
moments give tightness on compact sets in the marked space. This proves (14.4). Its unmarked
projection requires more than vague convergence: truncate the types, pass to the finite-type limit,
then let the cutoff grow using (14.11) and the convergent limiting series. This proves the stated
unmarked process. Finally replace qg∗ by qg . The type-two restriction gives (14.2) without assuming
a limit for n/g, by subsequences and compactness of [0, ∞]. Its zero-count probabilities give the
pants minimum law.

   For fixed T , take L = T / qg . Markov’s inequality shows that with probability 1 − O(T 2 /qg ) every
simple geodesic in this window is planar. Primitive geodesics shorter than 2 arcsinh 1 are simple,
by the standard collar theorem used in [41, Section 1.1]. Since qg → ∞, the full primitive process
and planar process agree on each bounded rescaled window with probability tending to one. This
transfers the process limit and its zero-count law to the systole.
   Finally restrict the first point to [η, T ] and finitely many cusp types. The first-point map is
continuous when its first length is unique and avoids the endpoints, as happens almost surely in
the limiting Poisson process. Let the type cutoff grow using (14.11), then η ↓ 0 and T → ∞. The
omitted length probabilities tend to zero by the zero-count law; W ≥ 1. Nonplanar competitors are
excluded on each fixed window. This also makes any tie rule immaterial and proves planarity of
every systolic curve with probability tending to one. The first-arrival density of the limiting marked
process is (14.5).                                                                                    □

boundary lengths ℓ, the collar lemma supplies this embedded half-collar. The function lies in H 1 (X),

complement of constants has dimension at least N − 1. Since the essential spectrum starts at 1/4,
the form version of min–max gives the claimed positive discrete eigenvalues. Apply (14.16) and let g

   The spectral mechanism is established in the fixed-genus work of Hide–Thomas [41, Theorem 1.10
and Remark 5.5]; their later bordered-volume asymptotics give linear counts below each fixed positive
threshold at fixed genus [40, Theorem 1.1]. Their deterministic result [42, Theorem 1.3], in the July

                                            √
to zero and the genus grows. In the range g ≪ n ≪ g, the corresponding second-moment volume
inputs already follow from Shen–Wu [79]. The extension supplied here is the density-dependent
intensity and removal of the upper restriction on n/g. No sharp eigenvalue asymptotic, matching
lower bound, or Poisson eigenvalue law is asserted.

14.4. The tight length spectrum. We now consider curves that remain noncontractible after
filling all cusps. The corresponding tight Weil–Petersson volumes were introduced by Budd [11].
Following Budd and Lions [13], a closed geodesic is tight if it is the unique shortest representative of
its free homotopy class on the filled surface. The distinction matters: several ordinary geodesics can
belong to the same class after the cusps are filled. The tight integration formula and the geometric
argument used in the proof are due to [13]; the new input is a volume estimate that allows arbitrary
prescribed cusp counts.
    Let Yλ satisfy λ(Yλ ) = λ, as in Proposition 9.4, and set

14.5. The full length spectrum at the critical cusp scale. The tight spectrum omits curves
                                                            √
that become contractible when the cusps are filled. At n ≍ g, these curves contribute on the same
length scale as the nonseparating curves. Their contribution is not Poisson: a two-cusp pair of pants
contributes its whole length spectrum, not only its boundary.
   For s > 0, let Ps be the hyperbolic pair of pants with two cusps and one geodesic boundary of
length s. Write Ks for its primitive unoriented length counting measure, including the boundary
once and excluding parabolic classes. Equal lengths are counted with their geometric multiplicities.

   Shen and Wu obtained the factorial-moment asymptotics for short two-cusp pants and the limiting
probability that such a curve occurs at this cusp scale [79, Lemmas 28 and 33]. The corresponding
Poisson mean is µc ([0, L]) = c2 (cosh(L/2) − 1)/(4π 2 ) on their stated short window. Theorem 14.6
concerns the full primitive process on every fixed length window, including the correlated lengths
inside each pair of pants. The background intensity and the geometric moment method come from
Mirzakhani and Petri [65]. The critical regime needs the volume bounds of Mirzakhani–Zograf
and Shen–Wu [66, 79] together with the zero-density case of Theorem 1.3 and Proposition 9.4: at
     √
n ≍ g the earlier estimates bound the genus ratio Vg−1,n+2 /Vg,n only from above. The full uniform
expansion is not used.
   The nonsimple part distinguishes this regime from closed surfaces. He, Shen, Wu and Xue proved
that the nonsimple systole of a Weil–Petersson random closed surface is asymptotic to log g, with a
more precise concentration about log g − log log g [35, Theorem 1.1]. Thus nonsimple curves leave
every fixed length window in that model. At the critical cusp scale here, two-cusp pants instead
retain nonsimple curves of bounded length.
   Outline of the proof. Short multicurves are organized by their cut graphs. Using only the volume
bounds of [66, 79], Lemma G.1 shows that in the limit a cut graph contributes only if it has
one component carrying the genus, possibly with nonseparating loops, and two-cusp pairs of pants
attached to it as leaves. A bounded-multiplicity argument (Lemmas G.2 and G.3) reduces intersecting
simple curves and nonsimple curves to such cut graphs; hence, with probability tending to one, every
short nonsimple curve lies in a two-cusp pair of pants. The mixed factorial moments of nonseparating
lengths and pants waists converge to those of independent Poisson processes (Lemma G.4, where the
zero-density volume ratios enter), and attaching to each waist the full spectrum of its pair of pants
gives Ξc . The details are given in Appendix G.
   The limit is not Poisson. If mL (s) = Ks ([0, L]), then Lemma G.2 and the support restriction
s ≤ 2L give finite moments and
                                                      Z
(14.22)               Var Ξc ([0, L]) − EΞc ([0, L]) = mL (s)(mL (s) − 1) µc (ds).
                           UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                   65


Besides its waist, Ps contains the primitive figure-eight around its two cusps, of length 2 arccosh(2 +
cosh(s/2)) by [76, Theorem 1.3]. For L > 2 arccosh 3, both lengths lie below L on an interval of
positive µc -measure. The right side of (14.22) is therefore strictly positive. The theorem identifies
these correlations in the limit; no convergence of the full-spectrum second moments is asserted.

                        15. Concluding remarks and open questions
   The results above show that one real parameter, determined by the cusp density, governs the
large-genus behavior of Vg,n in every regime. As n/g runs from zero to infinity, the saddle X(Yn/g )
moves from the fixed-cusp regime of Mirzakhani and Zograf to the Bessel singularity xc of the
fixed-genus theory of Manin and Zograf. The volume ratios, the boundary factor bY , the short-curve
intensities wk and the tight-length scale α interpolate in the same way.
   The coefficients of the normalized volume expansion are analytic in Y on a neighborhood of the
compact interval [0, y0 ]. This is the precise sense in which the two endpoint regimes fit into one
                                                                               √
expansion. It avoids the loss of uniformity of the fixed-n expansion at n ≍ g, while retaining an
error bound independent of n.
                                                            √              √
   The short geometry nevertheless changes at the scale g. For n = o( g), Shen and Wu proved
                                                   √
a uniform spectral gap [79, Theorem 3]. At n/ g → c ∈ (0, ∞), the primitive length spectrum
is a Poisson background together with independent clusters formed by two-cusp pairs of pants
(Theorem 14.6). Above this scale, short curves bounding genus-zero subsurfaces containing cusps
have the statistics of Theorem 14.1, and the spectral gap closes (Corollary 1.6).
   The proof rests on three features that may be useful beyond Weil–Petersson volumes. Along
the string flow, the alternating κ1 –ψ formula becomes a recursion with nonnegative coefficients, so
that comparison and monotonicity arguments hold uniformly in the deformation parameter. The
deformation ends at pure ψ intersection numbers, where Painlevé I fixes the integration constants
without any resurgence assumption. Finally, the positive convolution factor of Lemma 9.1 turns
estimates for generating functions into a local limit theorem that holds uniformly from bounded cusp
counts up to the critical cusp weight. The geometric consequences combine these volume estimates
with the integration formulas and short-curve arguments credited in Section 14.
15.1. Cusp weights and the JT spectral edge. Saad, Shenker and Stanford related the genus
expansion of Jackiw–Teitelboim (JT) gravity to a matrix integral, with Weil–Petersson volumes
as its geometric input [77]. The parameter Y describes a spectral edge in its defect deformation,
introduced by Maxfield–Turiaci and by Witten [59, 82]. We record the normalization to relate the
uniform estimates to that model. A defect of opening angle 2πα is a cusp at α = 0 [22, Section 2].
Let λdef be its weight, distinct from the cusp density λ = n/g, and use the convention 2γ = 1 of
Maxfield–Turiaci [59]. Their string equation at α = 0 and its branch connected to the undeformed
edge are           √
                     E0       p                               Y
                        I1 (2π E0 ) + λdef = 0,      E0 = − 2 ,      x = 2π 2 λdef .
                    2π                                        π
The Bessel expressions are interpreted by their entire power series. Substitution gives exactly
X(Y ) = x. Moreover, the definition (4.7) gives, for g ≥ 2,
                                   X      λn
                                      Vg,n def = (2π 2 )3g−3 Φg (x).
                                           n!
                                   n≥0
Thus a fixed cusp count is a coefficient of the defect-weighted amplitude, whereas Y parametrizes its
spectral edge.
  The genus-zero density, with its overall topological factor removed, can be written using our kernel.
Specializing the Abel integral in [59, equation (3.50)] to α = 0 gives

The square-root edge has become a cubic critical edge, the local geometry associated with the (2, 3)
critical point. This identifies the physical meaning of the same endpoint at which the normalized
intersection recursion reduces to pure ψ intersections.
    The exponent in the large-genus action agrees with this critical geometry. With δ = y0 − Y , the
endpoint expansions of Section 3 give
                                         √                          √
             b 2                        2 3 b 5/2              29/4 3                    √
                                                                       (xc − x)5/4 1 + O( xc − x) .
                                                                                                   
  xc − x = δ (1 + O(δ)),         Z=          δ (1 + O(δ)) =        1/4
             2                            5                     5b
The marked expansion in Theorem 8.2 uses the full action Z(Y ) and remains uniform as Y approaches
y0 . Replacing
         √     the action by its leading critical power would instead introduce a multiplicative factor
exp(O(g xc − x)) for fixed marking count. That replacement is not uniform in an arbitrary joint
limit. In formal genus counting, the corresponding balance is between the string coupling ℏ and
(xc − x)5/4 ; the genus index g is not the inverse string coupling. A double-scaled sum over all
genera requires a separate summation prescription and estimates beyond the large-genus coefficient
asymptotics proved here.
15.2. Open questions. We conclude with some questions that the present methods leave open.
   Actual Gevrey-1 remainders. The most direct analytic strengthening would replace the squared
factorial in Theorem 1.1 by a single factorial, with constants uniform in the cusp count and truncation
order K ≤ cg. A sufficiently small linear truncation would then give error O(e−ag ). The pure-ψ
endpoint already has this order of remainder (Subsection C.9), but transporting it across the whole
parameter interval requires sharper estimates for the actual recursion. In the present proof the
squared factorial comes from the degree paths in the profile recursion (Subsection 8.2); bounds on
the formal coefficients alone would not settle the question, since the actual remainders must be
controlled as well.
   Rate for adjacent ratios. Theorem 1.4 has the uniform error O(g −1/8 ), and Oε (g −1/4 ) for k ≥ εg;
neither exponent is claimed to be optimal (Appendix E). What is the optimal uniform rate?
   Other backgrounds. The derivation of the recursion (4.8) uses only the string, dilaton and Dijkgraaf–
Verlinde–Verlinde equations, and applies to other backgrounds of Kontsevich–Witten times; the
higher Weil–Petersson volumes of Kaufmann, Manin and Zagier arise in this way [48, 58]. For
which backgrounds does the deformed recursion have nonnegative coefficients, and does the uniform
analysis of this paper then extend?
   Several boundary components. Can one obtain relative asymptotics for mixed intersection numbers
when both the number of nonzero degrees and their sum grow with the genus, uniformly over their
allocation among the markings? Proposition 9.4 treats a fixed number of bounded nonzero lengths;
it does not provide this control of individual mixed intersections.
   Geometry and spectrum. Theorem 14.4 determines the tight spectrum at every cusp density, while
                                                                                √
Theorem 14.6 identifies the pants clusters in the ordinary spectrum when n/ g has a positive finite
limit. What is the joint distribution of ordinary geodesics in the same homotopy class after filling
the cusps, when n is proportional to g? Our spectral results are upper bounds for small eigenvalues;
their limiting distribution would require additional geometric input.

Proof of Lemma B.1. The lower bound. We prove the same lower bound for positive-genus tuples
with at most two zero entries, by induction on complexity. For a tuple with z ∈ {1, 2} zeros, the
total string weight is (3h − z)/(3h − 3) ≥ 1. String lowers complexity without increasing the number
of zeros. For a positive tuple, remove the ones by dilaton. Unless the tuple reduces to the genus-one
base, its genus is at least two. Its merger children are positive and its handle children have positive
genus and at most two zeros. Dropping the nonnegative separating terms in (B.2) therefore proves
the lower bound.

   This appendix proves Theorems 8.1 and 8.2. Subsections C.1–C.3 compute one further term in the

number p of distinguished markings remains fixed. Subsections C.4 and C.9 then construct the full
series and control its remainder as the truncation order grows.

   We next retain the leading separating terms of the adjacent difference. If the smaller component
has genus j and contains s of the other external labels, the complementary component has complexity
h − (2j + s). Stability forces 2j + s ≥ 2. Equality occurs only for (j, s) = (0, 2) and (1,  0). The
                                                                                          z
first component is a three-marked sphere, so its three degrees must be zero; it gives 2 T2−t . The
second is a one-marked torus, whose W amplitudes are θ1 /2 and 1/2 at degrees zero and one. It
gives (θ1 /2)T2−t + (1/2)T3−t . The two orientations cancel the factor 1/2 in the recursion. In both
cases the large all-zero component divided by the parent is A2 /h2 + Op (h−3 ), by fixed iterations of
(7.7) and (C.5). Its degrees can be replaced by zero at cost Op ((D + ℓ + 1)2 /h).
   After removing these two types the all-zero separating mass is Op (h−3 ). To verify this sharper
bound, repeat the proof of (6.12): the remaining genus-zero terms have s ≥ 3, the remaining genus-
one terms have s ≥ 1, and j ≥ 2 starts at h−4 . The same factorial-geometric series sums these bounds,
with any fixed polynomial in j; (6.13) handles the macroscopic splits. At p = 1, insert 2j + 1 cusps
in the larger component before raising its genus to marking count two, then use Wg,2 /Wg,1 = O(g).
This bounds its ratio by O((C/g)2j ). After the two genus-one orientations are removed, the factorial
series starts at j = 2 and is O(h−4 ). This argument uses the marking-independent constant in
(6.8). Pair counts and the moments of wℓ give an adjacent-difference error Op ((D + 1)3 /h3 ) for the
separating part.

the linear prefactor and degree count, is Op ((D + ℓ + 1)5 /h3 ). Above the cutoff use 0 ≤ c ≤ 1 and
polynomial growth of the displayed coefficients. Since the uniform moments (6.2) of wℓ are finite
to every order, these tails are Op (h−3 ), even after summing at most D increments. The dimension
cutoff is farther away and is covered by the same argument. Summing increments proves (C.4). For
a fixed tuple, expansions along two paths must have the same h−2 coefficient as g → ∞, proving
path independence.                                                                                     □

C.4. The formal hierarchy. We now construct the full series and control its remainder as the
truncation order grows. These are still estimates for generating functions: the passage to a prescribed
cusp count is made in Subsection 10.1 and Appendix D.
   The recursion is triangular. Each new degree coefficient uses earlier cusp and genus coefficients; the
dilaton and KdV equations then give the new cusp and genus coefficients. Proposition C.3 bounds
the coefficients and the actual remainders. Matching at the pure-ψ endpoint in Subsection C.9
completes the proof of Theorem 8.2.
76                                      ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


  The proof makes the heuristic of Section 8 precise. It divides each order-j coefficient by Sj2j ,
where Sj = D + p + M j is the weight described there. After this division, the coefficient rules
become coefficientwise inequalities between power series with nonnegative coefficients, and an analytic
majorant (Lemma C.2) bounds the normalized coefficients by CB j . The true remainders are then
controlled by a second, linear majorant system with the same normalization.
  Use a formal variable t for inverse complexity, and put

  The coefficient and remainder bounds below are obtained by comparing coefficientwise inequalities
with an analytic system. We isolate the elementary principle used for this comparison.
Lemma C.2 (Analytic majorants). Let Φ = (Φ1 , . . . , Φm ) be power series in (t, v1 , . . . , vm ) with
nonnegative coefficients, convergent near the origin, such that Φ(0, 0) = 0 and the matrix ∂v Φ(0, 0)
is strictly lower triangular. Then U = Φ(t, U ) has a unique formal solution with U (0) = 0. Its
coefficients arePnonnegative, and it converges near t = 0. Let ei,j ∈ [0, ∞], for 1 ≤ i ≤ m and j ≥ 1,
and put Ei = j≥1 ei,j tj . If ei,j ≤ [tj ]Φi (t, E) for all i, j, then ei,j ≤ [tj ]Ui . In particular every ei,j
is finite, and ei,j ≤ Cρ−j with C, ρ > 0 depending only on Φ.
Proof. Order the pairs (j, i) lexicographically. Since E has no constant term, [tj ]Φi (t, E) is a
polynomial with nonnegative coefficients in the numbers ek,l . A monomial ta v β with a ≥ 1 or |β| ≥ 2
contributes only coefficients with l < j, since each factor has order at least one. The remaining
terms are ∂vk Φi (0, 0) ek,j , and they occur only for k < i. Thus [tj ]Φi (t, E) involves only pairs
preceding (j, i). The equations U = Φ(t, U ) therefore determine the coefficients of U recursively
and uniquely, and these are nonnegative. The same induction and the monotonicity of polynomials
with nonnegative coefficients give ei,j ≤ [tj ]Ui . The derivative of (t, v) 7→ v − Φ(t, v) with respect to
v at the origin is I − ∂v Φ(0, 0), which is invertible. The analytic implicit-function theorem gives
a solution holomorphic near t = 0 and vanishing at t = 0; its Taylor series is the formal solution.
Cauchy’s estimate on a small circle gives the final bound.                                               □
Proposition C.3 (Uniform recursive expansions). There are C, B, M > 0 and one complex neigh-
borhood Ω of [0, y0 ] such that the preceding rules determine path-independent holomorphic coefficients
with

For every stable state p ≥ 1, h = 2g − 2 + p ≥ 1, every L ≥ 1, and every nonnegative degree tuple,
uniformly for 0 ≤ Y ≤ y0 ,

                                      cg,p (d) −         ep,j (d)h−j ≤ CB L (D + p + M L)2L h−L ,
                                                   j<L
(C.19)

                         j<L                           j<L

              di , Cp,j = [tj ]Cp (t) and Gp,j = [tj ]Gp (t); invalid-degree profiles and invalid genus
             P

   The proof of Proposition C.3 occupies Subsections C.5–C.8. At each order the construction runs
in the sequence profile, cusp ratio, genus ratio at p ≥ 2, and descent to p = 1; every profile on the
right of (C.15)–(C.16) has smaller order, and the dilaton and KdV rules use the current profile only
after it has been constructed. Subsection C.5 fixes the analytic neighborhood and the weights Sj
and records four elementary bounds. Lemma C.4 bounds the formal coefficients, uniformly in the
78                                         ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


order, by a system of analytic majorants; this is the first assertion of the proposition. Lemmas C.5
and C.6 control the omitted topological types and give first-order estimates for the true quantities.
Finally, Subsection C.8 proves the remainder bounds (C.19) by induction on the order, using a linear
majorant system for the normalized errors.

Gp,j that are holomorphic on Ω, and there are C, B0 such that, on Ω, for all p ≥ 1, all degree tuples

C∗ = 1 + v2 , H∗ = 1 + v3 , G∗ = 1 + v4 , the right-hand sides of (C.29) are power series in (t, v)
with nonnegative coefficients, convergent near the origin and vanishing there. At t = 0 the first
equation has no linear term, the second and third are linear in v1 only, and the fourth depends
only on v2 , v3 ; so the Jacobian at the origin is strictly lower triangular, and Lemma C.2 shows
that the formal solution of (C.29) converges near t = 0. The inequalities (C.25)–(C.28) now give
εj ≤ [E∗ ]j , γj ≤ [C∗ ]j , ηj ≤ [H∗ ]j and ζj ≤ [G∗ ]j for all j, by induction on j in the stage order: at
order j the right-hand sides involve η̄ only through orders below j, or through ηj in the descent,
and max(ηi , ζi ) ≤ [G∗ ]i as soon as ηi ≤ [H∗ ]i and ζi ≤ [G∗ ]i . Cauchy’s estimate for the convergent
solution gives εj , γj , ηj , ζj ≤ CB0j for all j, with C, B0 depending only on the system.              □

RL ≤ [A0 F + A0 (KE + KH + KG )R∗ ]L = [R∗ ]L by monotonicity of series with nonnegative
coefficients; the case L = 1 is the base estimate. Cauchy’s estimate for R∗ gives RL ≤ CB L
and proves (C.19); no infinite inverse-complexity series is evaluated. For each fixed degree tuple,
two coordinate paths give asymptotic expansions of the same ratio, and uniqueness of asymptotic
coefficients as g → ∞ proves the asserted path independence.
   Finiteness of the coefficient rules. It remains to justify the finite coefficient rule and
polynomial bounds used above. At each fixed order, the degree lattice admits a finite boundary
stratification: each coordinate is either one of finitely many low integers or lies above that threshold.
The coefficients are polynomial on every stratum. A merger has an exceptional low child degree only
when di + dj + ℓ − 1 is bounded; otherwise polynomial summation uses finitely many w moments.
For a nonseparating pair, separate bounded u, bounded v, and the interior. The interior sum is a
Faulhaber polynomial in u + v; overlap of the two boundary ranges adds finitely many exceptions. In
a retained split, dimension bounds u and dI by 3j + s − 2, so the same argument applies to the large
child. Summing the coordinate path preserves this class. One admissible (not optimal) choice of the
84                                       ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


low-degree thresholds is given by bdeg
                                     0  = 0 and bdeg      deg
                                                  K = 2bK−1 + 2K + 4. The adjacent coefficient has
degree at most 2K − 1, and its path sum has degree at most 2K, proving the claimed growth. The
single-degree specialization in dilaton is eventually polynomial; its sum uses finitely many signed bd
moments and low bd . Exact small amplitudes are finite polynomials in the θℓ . These facts reduce
every displayed infinite sum in the coefficient algorithm to finite kernel data. The common analytic
disks and removable critical normalization give analyticity of that data throughout [0, y0 ].       □

            Appendix D. Coefficient extraction with inverse-genus errors
   This appendix proves Theorems 10.1 and 10.2. Subsection D.1 makes the local limit theorem
of Section 9 quantitative; its complex comparison and Fourier estimates are reused afterwards.
Subsections D.2–D.4 prove Theorem 10.1, and Subsections D.5–D.7 prove Theorem 10.2 and compute
its first coefficient.
D.1. A quantitative local limit. We first make the local limit theorem (9.7) quantitative. The
argument transfers a real-axis error to a complex neighborhood and applies Fourier inversion; the
positive convolution factor controls the remaining part of the integral. This gives a power-rate
bound. The complex comparison and Fourier estimates established in the proof will also be used
in Subsection D.2, where retaining a Poisson factor gives an inverse-genus error uniformly over all
coefficient indices.

Factor es1 hz from the model. Its retained multiplier is 1 + hs2 z 2 + d1 z + Lp,1 (0)/h. Analytic

D.5. The analytic phase change at zero. To obtain an expansion that is uniform down to
k = 0, we change variables so that the phase becomes exactly that of a Poisson coefficient, following
Temme [80].
Lemma D.5 (Analytic phase change). Let S be analytic near zero with S(0) = 0 and S ′ (0) = s > 0,
and let x = x(ν) be the solution of xS ′ (x) = ν near zero, so that x = ν/s + O(ν 2 ). There is a jointly

into the stated remainder at every allowed order.

                 Appendix E. The rate for adjacent coefficient ratios
   This appendix proves Theorem E.1, which contains the rate in Theorem 1.4. We quantify the
three degree ranges treated in Section 13. The logarithmic comparison and concavity give the rate
away from the lowest degrees, including bounded positive codimension at the top. Bracket difference
estimates supply the remaining range. Balancing the bounds gives O(g −1/8 ) uniformly; we do not
claim that this exponent is optimal. The coefficients and the exact density are those of Theorem 1.4.
Theorem E.1 (Quantitative adjacent ratios). There is a constant C such that, for all sufficiently
large g,
                                         ag,k /ag,k+1
(E.1)                              sup                − 1 ≤ Cg −1/8 .
                                0≤k<3g−2  4π 2 /t2g,k
For every fixed ε ∈ (0, 3) there is Cε such that
                                         ag,k /ag,k+1
(E.2)                             sup         2 /t2
                                                      − 1 ≤ Cε g −1/4 .
                               εg≤k<3g−2  4π      g,k
In the second supremum k is an integer. Both bounds include bounded positive codimension from the
top coefficient.
98                                    ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


E.1. The logarithmic model up to its endpoints. We first make the logarithmic comparison of
Section 13 quantitative, up to both ends of the degree range.
  Use H, A em , µ from (13.4) and (13.10). Let ζ(u) be the inverse of µ(z) on 0 ≤ u ≤ 3/2, with
ζ(0) = 0. Since µ(z) = z/5 + O(z 2 ) at zero and µ′ (z) > 0 throughout [0, π 2 /2], the function
                                    χ(u) = u/ζ(u),             χ(0) = 1/5,
is positive and analytic in a neighborhood of the closed real interval. Write f for the function in
(13.14), extended continuously to [0, 3], and set
(E.3)      q(α) = 4αχ((3 − α)/2),     f ′ (α) = − log q(α),         |f ′′ (α)| ≤ C(1 + α−1 ) (0 < α ≤ 3).

   The argument is a quantitative version of the one used for the leading equivalent, based on the
same logarithmic comparison and concavity. Liu–Xu’s fixed-codimension expansion has finer errors
at each fixed codimension [55]; the bounds here hold with a single constant across all valid degrees.

Proof of Theorem 14.4. For fixed disjoint intervals in (0, ∞), count ordered tuples of simple, pairwise

   Here the disjoint-tuple moments must be related to the actual count moments: a small probability
of intersections alone would not suffice. Choose L above all interval endpoints, and let AL be
the event that scaled lengths at most L include a self-intersecting tight curve or two intersecting
                            UNIFORM LARGE-GENUS VOLUME ASYMPTOTICS                                     103


tight curves. Write N      br for the disjoint-tuple count in the prescribed intervals, with multiplicities
r = (r1 , . . . , rj ). We use the deterministic constructions in the proofs of [13, Propositions 5.2–5.3],
rather than their probabilistic conclusions in the near-critical model. These constructions apply
after rescaling by any positive length scale.
   Fix ε > 0 below the interval endpoints. On AL , outside the event of a tight systole shorter than ε,
the construction extends a counted tuple to a separating tight multicurve. Its number of components
and scaled lengths are bounded in terms of ε, L, r, independently of g, n. If there is a shorter tight
systole, either it adds a disjoint curve in [0, ε], or the tuple again extends to a separating multicurve.
The original ordered tuple is retained in these extensions. Thus the separating terms vanish by (F.6),
and the mixed disjoint-tuple limit gives

where Ii are the prescribed intervals. Letting ε ↓ 0 removes the right side. The same construction
without a marked tuple gives P(AL ) → 0. Conditional on AcL , factorial counts are exactly disjoint-
tuple counts. Their limiting moments therefore remain unchanged, and the moment-determinate
Poisson law gives joint count convergence.
  The first-moment bound for simple tight curves in [0, ε] tends to ν([0, ε]) → 0. A shortest tight
curve is simple, so the same bound controls the event of any tight curve in this window. This
extends the convergence to [0, ∞) and gives tightness on compact intervals. Finally the disjoint-tuple
moments bound the number of curves in a fixed window in probability. For any fixed upper bound
on that number, (F.6) rules out every disconnected complement. Letting the upper bound increase
proves the last claim.                                                                              □

G.1. Ordinary cuts and cusp allocations. Short multicurves are organized by their cut graphs:
the vertices are the components of the complement, labeled by their genera, and the edges are the
curves. Lemma G.1 bounds the expected number of multicurves with a given cut graph. It shows
that, in the limit, only nonseparating curves and boundaries of two-cusp pairs of pants contribute;
the later subsections compute these contributions jointly.

type (0, 1) needs at least two cusps, and one of type (0, 2) at least one.

G.2. Excluding complex short subsurfaces. We need expectation bounds, not only a bound on
the probability of a bad configuration, so we give the multiplicity argument explicitly.
Lemma G.2 (Bounded primitive count). For fixed K, L, a finite-area hyperbolic surface S with
geodesic boundary and 0 < −χ(S) ≤ K has at most C(K, L) primitive unoriented geodesics of length
at most L, uniformly in its metric.
Proof. Double S along its boundaries. The double has area at most 4πK. Fix a Margulis constant
ε > 0. A primitive geodesic wholly in a thin component is its core; there are boundedly many such
cores. Every other geodesic meets the thick part. A maximal ε/4-separated net there has bounded
cardinality by area packing. At each net point, orbit points of a lift are uniformly separated, so
hyperbolic ball packing bounds the number of based loops of length at most L + ε. Connecting a
short geodesic to a net point proves the assertion. Primitivity is preserved upon inclusion in the
double or in an ambient surface: a root would have the same geodesic image and would already be a
closed curve in S.                                                                              □
                                                                                √
Lemma G.3 (Complex short configurations). Fix r and L. As g → ∞ with n/ g → c ∈ (0, ∞),
(G.3)       E[intersecting ordered simple r-tuples of lengths at most L] = Or,L,c (g −1/2 ),

(G.4) E[nonsimple primitive curves of length at most L not filling two-cusp pants] = OL,c (g −1/2 ),
and the expected number of simple separating curves of length at most L that are not waists of
two-cusp pairs of pants tends to zero.
Proof. For fixed r, L, a tuple of r distinct simple geodesics of length at most L has bounded total
intersection number. We use the local segment argument in the proof of [65, Proposition 4.5]; it
applies equally in the presence of cusps. Thicken each nonannular component of their union, fill
complementary disks and peripheral once-punctured disks, and tighten the essential boundaries.
The resulting pieces have bounded topology and total boundary length at most 2rL. Keep one
ambient geodesic for each parallel boundary class. Empty annuli between parallel copies collapse;
their two incidences are the two sides of a single edge, possibly a loop. An annulus with cusps
does not collapse and is a (0, 2) component counted among the v vertices. Isolated tuple curves are
retained as additional cut edges, unless they are already one of the tightened boundaries. Minimal
intersection ensures that these boundaries are disjoint from the original tuple.
   There are finitely many resulting graph shapes and markings at fixed r, L. For a fixed cut
multicurve, Lemma G.2 bounds the choices of original curves in each marked piece. Hence the tuple
count is bounded pointwise by a constant times a finite sum of marked cut-multicurve counts. An
intersecting simple tuple cannot fill two-cusp pants. Its marked component either has hi ≥ 1, forcing
106                                  ZHIYI LI AND QIHANG WANG


a second stable vertex to carry the unbounded genus, or has type (0, 2), or has type (0, 1) with at
least three cusps. The bounds in Lemma G.1 give (G.3).
   For a single nonsimple primitive curve of length at most L, use the filling construction of [37,
Definition 4.10 and Lemma 4.11]. The filled piece has boundary length at most 2L and area at most
4L, so its topology is bounded. The same recording retains formal boundary sides that tighten to
the same ambient curve as sides of a cut edge. Lemma G.2 controls the number of curves filling
each piece. The preceding classification therefore gives (G.4). A filled piece cannot be the whole
ambient surface for large g, since its topology is bounded. Finally, the one-edge case of Lemma G.1
shows that simple separating curves of bounded length other than two-cusp pants waists vanish in
expectation.                                                                                     □
G.3. Joint nonseparating and pants counts. Let Ng count simple nonseparating lengths and
let Qg count two-cusp pants waist lengths.
                                                 √
Lemma G.4 (Joint Poisson limit). As g → ∞ with n/ g → c ∈ (0, ∞),
(G.5)                                   (Ng , Qg ) =⇒ (N , Qc )
jointly in the vague topology on [0, ∞), where N and Qc are independent Poisson point processes of
intensities ν and µc .
Proof. In the mixed factorial moments of (Ng , Qg ), (G.3) removes intersecting tuples. Lemma G.1
removes every disjoint cut type except the graph with a single large component, k nonseparating
loops and r two-cusp leaves. For prescribed bounded intervals for the k + r lengths, its exact
contribution is

(G.6)                            V g−k,n−r+2k (s, t, t, 0     )   s j dsj   ti dti .

Here (n)2r is the falling factorial. The factor 2−r counts unordered pairs of cusp labels at the ordered
pants leaves, as in [79, Lemma 28]; 2−k exchanges the sides of each nonseparating cut, as in [65,
Proposition 4.2]. There is no factor r! or k!, since both tuples are ordered.
  The volume ratio is a product of r cusp ratios and k genus ratios. The cusp ratios satisfy
Vg,j+1 /Vg,j ∼ 8π 2 g for j ≤ n by [66, Lemma 5.1(ii)], since n = o(g). For the genus ratios, [66,

case B(0) = 1 of Theorem 1.3. Together,

All mixed factorial moments on disjoint bounded intervals converge to those of independent Poisson
counts. Higher fixed moments give uniform integrability, and the multivariate Poisson law is moment
determinate. The first-moment bounds near zero are O(ε2 ). This proves the lemma.                □

G.4. Proof of Theorem 14.6.
Completion of the proof of Theorem 14.6. Fix L < ∞. By Lemma G.3, with probability tending to
one every nonsimple primitive curve of length at most L fills two-cusp pants with waist at most 2L.
By (G.3), the pants waists up to 2L are pairwise disjoint with probability tending to one. The pants
interiors are then disjoint as well. Other simple separating curves up to L are absent with probability
tending to one. On this event, the full primitive counting measure restricted to [0, L] is exactly
                                                          
                                                 X
                                      Ng +             Ks       .
                                            s∈Qg ∩[0,2L]     [0,L]
Every interior primitive geodesic of Ps is nonsimple and fills it. The filling boundary bound implies
s ≤ 2ℓ for such a geodesic of length ℓ. Thus pants with waist greater than 2L cannot contribute,
and the limiting sum is locally finite.
   For ε > 0, the family Ps , ε ≤ s ≤ 2L, is compact in its fixed-topology moduli space. Lengths
of marked classes vary continuously, and only finitely many such classes have length at most L
somewhere on this compact family. For the latter assertion, use a finite cover by uniformly bilipschitz
markings, with standard neighborhoods at the two cusps, and the finiteness of each center metric’s
length spectrum below a fixed bound. Hence s 7→ ⟨f, Ks ⟩ is continuous away from zero for continuous
compactly supported f . Apply (G.5) with waist cutoff ε, choosing L larger than the support of
f . The probability of removing any waist is at most EQg ([0, ε]), whose limit is µc ([0, ε]) = O(ε2 ).
Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (14.20). The independent Poisson Laplace functionals give (14.21).              □

                                       Acknowledgements
   We thank Yang Shen for his valuable comments on the exposition and the discussion of prior
work. His suggestions prompted us to give a clearer account of the literature on Weil–Petersson
volume asymptotics, the geometry of moduli spaces, and the spectra of random hyperbolic surfaces,
and to clarify the relationship between our spectral applications and the earlier results and methods
of Shen and Wu.
   We thank Hao Xu for helpful correspondence concerning the work of Liu and Xu and its relationship
to the present paper. In particular, his comments prompted us to distinguish more clearly the
uniformity in the spectral-curve parameter at a fixed number of markings from the uniformity in the
total cusp count established here, and to explain that the determination of Zograf’s constant in our
proof does not require the latter uniformity. This exchange helped us improve the comparison of the
two deformation arguments and the description of their respective scopes.
   We acknowledge Gewu Intelligence Lab for providing the collaborative research environment in
which this project was developed.
